26: The Impacts of Natural Disasters on Educational Attainment:
27: Cross -Country Evidence from Macro Dat a.
59: Abstract
60: Do natural disasters impact educational attainment? Education as a paramount factor of economic development suffers from the
61: uncontrollable effects of these increasing events from storms to floods, earthquakes to wildfires. Globally, educational resources are destroyed ,
62: directly and indirectly, students and teachers are displaced or killed, parents’ income is affected because of natural disast ers. Investments in
63: human capital for rich - and low-income countries are exposed to the uneven impact of natural disasters that adjusts household and country -
64: level decisions, leaving them to short and long -run losses. Exploring the influence of natural disasters on secondary school attainment across
65: a sample of 85 countries from 1960 to 1990, we employ a panel data set from the Emergency Events dataset and Barro -Lee dataset. Using
66: year and country fixed effects, the data shows that the intensity of deaths from natural disasters has a greater effect on s econdary school
67: attainment than the intensity of damages from these disasters. Data also suggest s that damages per capita have a slightly significant effect on
68: the secondary school repetition rate. Psychological and behavioral effects caused by deaths from disasters lead to disinvestments from human
69: capital and these degenerates into long -term effects on economic development. Consequently, while natural disasters cannot be averted, its
70: damages can be curtailed, therefore, it is crucial to inform policies th at drive countries to a conscientious effort for high -performance social
71: intervention programs; and motivate an urgency for climate change conversation.
73: Keywords: Educational attainment, Natural Disasters, Economic Development, Education, Human Capital,
74: Disinvestments.

142: Several studies report tha t the gravity of disasters is defined by the economic development factors
143: such as the size of the government or public sector, level of educational attainment, openness, financial
144: markets, and so on (Skidmore and Toya, 2007; Noy 2009). They show that high levels of education,
145: openness, etc., exhibit readiness in the event of a disaster. Other authors have investigated the effect of
146: natural disasters on economic growth such as the Hsiang and Jina (2014) global study of cyclones. They
147: suggest that the long -term disaster costs set an average country back by two years of growth for every one
148: standard deviation in a year’s cyclone exposure and this lowers GDP by 3.6 percentage points twenty years
149: later. They go further to predict that by 2090, damages from natura l disasters would be as high as 9.7
152: Other studies are concerned with the sensitivity of these contemporaneous and disproportionate
153: effects for poor households in the events of typhoons in the Philippines (Anttila -Hughes and Hsiang,
158: while the child suffers hunger and ma lnutrition.
159: Furthermore, some authors discuss the natural disaster effects on not just cognitive skills but also
160: future earnings (Hoddinott et al, 2008), where household income cuts that shift children out of schools
161: into the labor market to help them cont ribute to the family income (Kousky, 2016). The likelihood of
162: natural disasters to affect educational attainment via health has also been widely discussed where in rural
163: households in Central Mexico, children are found to be more susceptible to (self -repor ted) diseases
164: because of weather -related (flood, drought , and hurricanes) and geological (earthquakes) shocks (De la
169: intensity of disaster damages and the number of deaths influence the disinvestment in education across
170: countries. Therefore, the objective of this study is to fully explore that relationship while answering
171: questions like, what are the impacts of damages and deaths per capita on secondary educational attainment
172: and repetition rates? Does the income of a household show variation in the impacts of these disasters?
173: We contribute to this literature by analyzing the gap in human capital created by natural disa sters.
174: Particularly, we look at the micro -level effects of natural disa sters on educational attainment using macro -
175: level data .
177: This study tries to motivate pol icy implications for better risk management and coping strategies
178: and fortifying the conversation about how much these risks as a subject of climate change can contribute
179: to the loss of education al opportunity for developing countries.
181: The rest of this pa per is organized as follows; Section 2 reviews disasters and relevant literature.
182: Section 3 covers the methodology: hypothesis, model specification , and data sources. Section 4 discusses
183: the results and Section 5 reviews the conclusion.
185: 2. Literature Revi ew
186: 2.1 Natural Disasters
187: Natural disasters are natural events resulting from the natural processes of the earth such as floods,
188: earthquakes, or hurricane leading to great damage or loss of life. For this paper, 8 major disasters will be
189: explored from the Emergency Events Database (EM -DAT) which is maintained by the Centre for
190: Epidemiology of Disasters (CRED) at the University of Louvalin, Belgium. Particularly, the number of
195: activity, landslides; hydro -meteorological and climatological such as storms, floods, droughts , and
196: wildfires. Each disaster is r ecorded as a disaster in the database if only at least: 10 or more people are
197: reported killed; 100 or more people are reported affected, injured and/or homeless; a declaration of a state
198: of emergency; and the case where the country calls for international assistance.
199: Figure 1, according to the CRED International Disaster Database, shows that natural disasters
200: events from 1960 to 1990 has increased from about 40 events to 296 events and as highs as 400 events in
201: the 2000s. Worldwide economic losses from natu ral disasters between 2003 and 2013 totals to about 1.5
202: trillion US dollars in damages. Subsequently , in 2015 and 2016, economic losses were about $92 billion
203: and $108 billion respectively. For the 1960 to 1990 period, about 218 million people were affecte d by
204: natural disasters per annum.
206: Floods have been ranked as the most frequent natural disaster, with 3,062 (about 43% of all events)
207: flood events recorded between 1995 and 2015 by CRED. Storms and earthquakes account for about 28%
208: and 8% (2,018 and 562 e vents) respectively. Landslide and Droughts are at 5%, that is 387 and 334 events.
209: Wildfire and Volcanic activit ies are 4% and 2% (251 and 111 events) of total recorded natural disasters
210: between 1995 and 2015 (World Economic Forum, 2016). From Figure 2, th e data for this paper shows
211: that USA, India, China and Indonesia had the highest number of disasters at 231(7.9%), 201 (6.9%), 148
212: (5.1%) and 133 (4.59%) events respectively. Japan and Bangladesh have 116 (4.0%) and 102 (3.5%)
213: occurrences recorded from 196 0 to 1990. Figure 3 presents the number of occurrences for 1960 to 1990
214: in percentages for 85 countries . Figures 4 -11 gives the distribution of disaster incidences around the world
215: by type .
218: 2.2 Natural Disasters and Human Capital
219: There has been a wide debate about the effects of natural disasters shocks which could either have
220: positive (Skidmore and Toya, 2002) or negative (Raddatz, 2007) effects on GDP. Both sides consider how
221: the destruction of capital (both physical and human) by natural disaster sho cks could either affect
222: productivity in the long -run or encourage replacement of outdated capital with newer technologies.
223: Parts of literature discuss the potential effect of natural disaster risks on mortality which lowers
224: education investment in disaster -prone countries (Cuaresma, 2010). According to the Asian Development
225: Bank and the World Bank, the 2005 Earthquake in Pakistan led to the death of 853 teachers and 18,095
242: keep these poor families from investing in human capital therefore partly justifying the slow rate of
243: education attainment in low -income countries. Economic behavior, here, implies that the level of
244: economic standing which determines preparedness in the event of disasters.
245: Studies have show n that d isaster -prone areas tend to have declining levels of growth. Cyclone -prone
246: climates affect the level of long -term growth and income for a country such that, cumulative effects of
247: cyclones depress a country’s per capita income by 7.4% two decades after the event (Hsiang and Jina,
248: 2014) . Tendencies for repeated incidences in these countries, such as small island states, affect growth .
249: Most of these island states are prone to tropical cyclones due to the comparatively extensive coastline.
250: The more the natural disasters, the higher it leads to high levels of damages for these states (Rasmussen,
251: 2004) .
263: 7
264: of natural disasters (loss of lives, destruction of properties) which decomposes into indirect effects such
265: as, macro impacts (general disequilibrium effects on prices and earnings, fiscal constraints) and micro
266: impacts (household level effects li ke inability to invest in human capital, high probability of disability and
269: accumulation. To fully connect how inconsistencies caused by disaster shocks are a threat to future
270: productivity and welfare through educational attainment, we must understand the theme supporting
271: educational attainment.
273: 2.3 Human Capital and Educational Attainment
274: Education has been described as a major contributor to growth. Human capital accumulation which
275: is explained with investments in education is a strong determinant of economic progress as seen in labor
276: productiv ity and social outcomes (Barro and Lee, 2013) and an abundance of well -educated human
277: resources can be hampered by various natural disaster shocks.
279: Educational attainment, as reflected in the enrollment of school -aged population, is indicative of past
280: inflows of schooling and the cumulative of these flows create future stocks of educational attainment (Lee
281: and Lee, 2016, Barro and Lee, 1993, 2001). Secondary school attainment, therefore, represents the level of
282: secondary attained, that is the percentage of an age -group that have completed their secondary education
283: and have some form of secondary schooling.
295: but also the r eturn to additional human capital investments. Income, in the sense that, if low -education
296: jobs offer higher wages, especially in poor countries then the opportunity cost of staying in school
297: increases. Overall, this causes inequality and slows long -term g rowth (Shah and Steinberg, 2017; Baez et
298: al, 2010).
308: environment, accessibility, instruction time, etc.) bolster the accumulation of human capital. For example,
309: in many developing countries, a Southern and Eastern Africa Consortium for Monitoring Educational
310: Quality (SACMEQ) study showed that the average child was in a school with 8.7 of the twenty -two
311: desirable school resource items; the range was from 4.3 items in Malawi to 16.7 in Seychelles, with wid e
312: urban -rural variation within countries (UNDP, 2005).
314: The level of attainment has also been connected to the ample or lack of supply of affordable
315: educational opportunities where the UNDP report in 2005 discovered that children who live close to a
316: second ary school had higher educational attainment. Furthermore, this report showed that children from
317: higher socio -economic backgrounds – where parents had more education and households had more
318: material possessions, including more books – tended to perform bet ter than those from poorer homes.
331: 2.3.1 THE INTUITION OF EDUCATIONAL ATTAINMENT IN H UMAN CAPITAL
333: The Barro -Lee (2011) study has successfully connected the importance of human capital attained
334: through education to economic growth. The higher the level and distribution of education people receive,
335: the higher the productivity. Lucas (1990) discusses that the growth rate of per capita income depends on
337: 9
340: to education would have potentially large effects on human capital accumulation and long -term growth
341: rate (Lucas, 1990) making it an easy link between human capital, productivity, and investments in human
342: capital accumulation.
343: Likewise, the Barro -Lee study (2010, 2016) in tandem with Lucas (1988) stress “the way an
344: individual allocates his time over various activities in the current period and how it affects his productivity
345: or his average skill level” in the formation of human capital. Therefore, the level and quality of human
347: accumulation of human capital (Lucas, 1988).
348: Therefore, using survey/census data gathered from United Nations Education al, Scientific and
349: Cultural Organization (UNESCO), Eurostat, national statistics agencies and other sources, the study
350: creates a 5-year interval of educational attainment measure for over 58 countries from 1960 to 1990. These
351: measures cover the distributio n of educational attainment for the adult population over the age of 15 and
352: over the age of 25 by gender for seven levels of schooling. Therefore, to prevent measurement error, the
353: authors utilize observations by 5 -year age intervals rather than the 5 -year periods applied in their previous
354: studies. Furthermore, the forward and backward extrapolation strategy is advanced by constructing
355: estimates for survival rates by age and by education; and completion ratios by educational attainment and
356: by age group. Thi s structure ensures that the available aggregate stock of human capital is satisfactorily
357: and concurrently measured as an input to production.
359: 2.4 Natural Disasters and Educational Attainment
360: The heterogeneous and likely disproportionate nature of disast ers make s the relationship between
361: natural disasters and school attainment quite ambiguous.
363: Theoretical evidence shows that the direct effects of these shocks such as the destruction of critical
372: earthquake in eastern Guatemala had a negative impact on the number of grades attained by adults born
373: in four villages of the country.
374: Furthermore, another link for the disaster -human -capital study is the shifts in parental investments in
375: children due to cuts in expenditure that seem to be temporary adjustments to shocks now become
383: Stress Disorder and depression (Kousky, 2016), which affect academic grade s, attendance and
384: subsequently, educational attainment.
385: Empirically, these shocks thereby affect the allocation of time in the formation of human capital.
386: This empirical discussion of the importance of the years of schooling in human capital formation (Barro -
387: Lee, 1993) gives the paper its foundation. The likeli hood of positive productivity shocks has been seen to
388: lower levels of educational attainment, just by a move from regular rainfall to a positive rainfall shock.
389: Such that wages increase by 2 percent math test scores decrease by 2 –7 percent of a standard de viation;
390: school attendance decreases by 2 percentage points; and the probability that a child is enrolled in school
400: An important issue in discussing the effects of disasters on educational attainment is the interplay
401: of the demand and supply effects of education in the period of the disaster. Several works of literature
413: in less -prone areas of disaster, then cases like this resolve in no significant amount of damages from
414: disasters on educational attainment. That is, combining both demand and supply effects, damage s from
415: disasters may not have a contemporaneous effect on attainment (Halliday, 2006; Rush, 2014).
416: An important concern in the disaster -human -capital literature is the death effects of disasters on
417: educational attainment. Loss of a parent or guardian is on e of the most traumatic events a child can face.
418: Several studies show that if the loss of a parent reduces investments in children, it can also have other
419: long-lasting implications. Using Indonesia's survey data during 1994 -1996, Gertler et al (2004) empir ically
420: show that the demographic change caused by the death of a parent can affect the investment in children’s
421: education. The rate at which one human capital investment factor, school enrollment, is largely affected
432: This goes alongside with studies that have shown that a higher level of female attainment stimulates the
433: acquisition of human capital by children while male educational attainment is more important in terms of
434: the direct effects on GDP growth and non -human investment. (Barro and Lee, 1993, 2001).
436: Overall, this study tries to contribute to the disaster -human capital study by investigati ng the effects
437: of natural disaster shocks on educational attainment for a broad group of countries.
439: 2.4.1 Repetition Rate
440: Although repetition rates could be classified as a measure of school performance, it is a crucial way
441: to investigate the efficiency of school systems in the face of natural disasters (UNESCO, 1993). Barro -Lee
442: (1993) describe repeaters as students who are a dmitted to the same level as the previous year. In compiling
443: data for this rate, differences in promotion standards must have been taken into consideration. This study
445: 12
446: will be using the proportion of repeaters in the secondary level of education. UNESCO re cords that out
447: of the 17 countries with the highest rates of secondary school repetition, 15 are in Sub -Saharan Africa and
448: the other 2 are in the Middle East and North Africa. The highest levels of secondary repetition rates are
449: recorded in Congo (30.8%), Iraq (27.5) and Algeria (27.2%) (UNESCO, UIS 2004). The presence of
450: psychological, social or health factors from natural disasters cause s lower attendance, lower performance,
451: substitution effects of a child’s time in school and working to earn household in come. These factors create
452: gaps in schooling, lower a child’s grade and lead to higher drop -out rates in the long run.
454: 3. METHODOLOGY
455: 3.1 Overview of Data and Data Sources
456: Empirically, this study aims to analyze the impact of economic damages and deaths from natural
457: disasters on educational attainment on all population and by gender for 85 countries from 1960 to 1990.
458: Therefore, following the works of Barro -Lee (2001, 2010) and Escaleras et al (2011), this study combines
459: data from the Emergency Events Database2, Barro -Lee Educational Attainment (1993)3 data set and the
460: World -Bank4 database to carry out this analysis.
461: Primarily, we limit analysis to only 8 of the most frequently occurring natural disasters; Floods,
462: Storms, Earthquakes, Landslides, Drought, Wildfire, Volcanic activity and Mass movement (Dry). All 8
463: disasters are categorized into two groups; geophysical and hydro -meteorological. This study considers the
465: disasters (total deaths) as the major predictor variables. With the goal of measuring the long -term effects
466: of natural disaster shocks , we use the intensity of deaths and damages as measures for disasters for the
467: current and a period after that. This will cover 5 and 10 years after the event has occurr ed. Despite the
468: misreporting or systematic reporting differences across countries and regimes, the CRED datab ase is the
469: most advantageous available.
471: The Barro -Lee dataset, as earlier discussed, provides data set for the educational attainment data
472: of 85 countries by sex and by 5 -year age intervals. We use the 15-24 population at the secondary school
474: 2 The Emergency Events Database is the International Disasters Database maintained by the Centre for Research on
475: the Epidemiology of Disasters (CRED) at the University of Louvalin, Belgium. The data can be downloaded from
476: www.emdat.be/
478: 3 The Barro -Lee Dataset provides educational attainment for 5 -year intervals. The dataset can be downloaded from
479: http://www.barrolee.com
481: 4 The World Bank Open Databank provides an extensive collati on of data and can be downloaded from
482: https://data.worldbank.org
484: 13
485: attainment a nd repetition rates as main dependent variables. We use the empirical foundation of the Barro -
486: Lee (1993, 2001) study specifie d;
488: Q = Q(F, R) + ε ,
490: to control for other factors that influence educational attainment. F represents family resources (which
491: include parents’ income and education) and R represents school resources (such as government
492: expenditure per student and pupil -teacher ratio). Therefore, we use the World Bank database to provide
493: data on constant Gross Domestic Product per capita (GDPpc) p er capita as a proxy for parents’ income
494: and aggregate country population. The Barro -Lee (1993) dataset provides data on the repetition rate and
495: secondary school attainment, government spending per student, pupil -teacher ratio and parents’ education.
497: Acco rding to the World Bank’s country classification, all countries in my sample are grouped by
498: region into East Asia and Pacific, Europe and Central Asia, Latin America and the Caribbean, the Middle
499: East and North Africa, North America, South Asia and Sub -Saharan Africa. Countries are also grouped
500: by income into Low -income, Lower -middle -income, Upper -middle -income and High -income economies.
501: All variables are measured in percentages which allow for consistency of empirical estimation of this study.
504: The main question for this study;
505: • Do natural disasters impact human capital formation by reducing educational attainment?

533: 3.3 Empirical Strategy
534: The study applies the fixed effects model to measure the impacts of natural disasters on educational
535: attainment following the results of a Durbin -Wu Hausman test. The results from the test show that the
536: fixed effect is more aligned to yield consistent efficient estimates as compared to the random effects model.
537: Specifically, we employ a year and country fixed effects model to account for time - and country -specific
538: factors , cluster ed at the country level. The time -specific fixed effec ts accoun t for any movement in the
539: educational attainment variable for all countries while the country -specific fixed effects control for
540: unobserved country variation that remain s unchanged over time. Each regression was estimated with
541: robust standard erro rs to check for issues of error variation (heteroscedasticity). Therefore, conditional on
542: the country and year fixed effects, we assume that natural disasters are exogenous ly determined . Variation
543: is identified by a within -country variation over time.
545: Since approximately all countries in the dataset have many disasters across the same year such that
546: many countries appeared with repeated time values, data collected for natural disasters is collapsed to
547: match the 5 -year age interval educational attainment data. This ensures that a panel data set is available for
548: analysis.
553: 15
554: Tables 2A to 2J in Appendix 2 provides a description of statistics for the full sample of 85
555: countries, 21 developed countries and 64 developing countries, and across regions. Secondary education
556: attainment contains the value of those who have attained any form of secondary education and drop -out
557: rate inclusive (Barro -Lee, 1993). This explains the average of the 15+ and 25+ population secondary
558: school attainment at 22.74% and 17.6% for the developing countries and 21.55% and 16.6% for developed
559: countries. I ncluding the high drop -out rates, which at maximum 80% in developing countries as compared
560: to 68.8% in developing countries, would make secondary attainment rates higher than it seems. Figure 3
561: gives a very detailed description of the secondary school atta inment across countries. The repetition rates,
562: on average, are highest in sub -Saharan and South Asia at 10% and 13% respectively. Barro -Lee (1993)
563: point out that the promotion standard of schools in a country reflects the quality of schools in that country .
564: Data shows as the teacher to student ratio go as high as 42 students per class in developing as compared
565: to 37 students in developed countries. Public spending per student suggests that developed countries spend
566: about $772 per student and developing coun tries. Gross Domestic Product per capita is at its lowest for
567: South Asia at about $400 and $1777 for Sub -Saharan Africa. Deaths are on average 11,464 people in 85
568: countries with damages of over $500,000 on average for the entire sample.
570: 4.2 Regression Res ults
571: 4.2.1 Deaths and Secondary School Attainment
572: The regression results from c olumns 1 and 2 of Table 3 (see Appendix) estimate the effect s of the
573: intensity of deaths on secondary school attainment using both country and year fixed effects . We see that
574: in the current period of the disasters , there is a negative and significant association with deaths per capita
575: and secondary school attainment. This result is consistent with both columns 1 and 2 whether or not
595: These results also portray the importance of parents’ education for children’s education (Gitter
596: and Braham, 2006 ; Barro -Lee, 1993 ). The outcome of a child’s education is reflective that of the parent
597: and this relevant for the human capital accumulation of that child. In the event of a disaster, the more
598: withdrawal of investment is reduced, the higher the educational attainment of the c urrent generation which
599: translates into higher educational attainment for future generations.
601: 4.2.2 Damages and Secondary School Attainment
602: For Table 4 , we use the same model specification with the intensity of economic damages as th e
603: two main predictor variables with country and year fixed effects. Co lumns 1 and 2 show a negative and no
604: significant relationship between damages per capita and secondary school attainment. Data suggests that
605: the damages per capita have no significant impact on secondary edu cational attainment. We see the
606: consistent significance of parents’ education for secondary school attainment which remains positive and
610: of education in the event of a disaster. Halliday (2006) and Rush (2014) discuss the likelihood of current
611: effects of damages on educational attainment to fade away when the distorted supply of educational
612: resources is met with an inelastic demand of education by parents . This is mostly seen in the migration
613: effects caused by natural disasters. We see that the direction of damages per capita on educational
614: attainment is consistent with the effects of deaths, but this is not statistically significant .
615: Educational attainment serves as a long -term element of education. We see the effects of both
616: disaster measures on the highest number of years of schooling in the long run.
617: The use of damages as a measure somewhat poses a potential threat to validity. This is hard to
620: 17
621: disaster depends on the size of the country in terms of per capita income5 (Cavallo et al, 2009). Cyclical
622: fluctuations in relation to a country’s gross domestic product (GDP) affects the over -estimation or
623: under -estimation of economic damages that may occur. From Figure 1 (see Appendix), we see that a
624: clear representation of the cyclical fluctuation across many countries over many years . The effect of
625: these fluctuations reflects the systematic differences and misreporting of damages as a measure. This
626: poses a threat of endogeneity in our analysis of natural disasters using economic damages.
628: 4.2.3 Disasters and Repetition Rate
630: on the rate at which children repeat grades in school. Table 5 represent s the same model specification with
631: secondary school repetition rates as the dependen t variable. From columns 1 and 2, data suggests that a
632: positive association between repetit ion rates and natural disasters, with higher and significant effects from
633: damages per capita in the current period of the disaster . Secondary educational attainment decreases by
634: 19.6% for a 1% increase in damages per c apita. We see consistency in the direction of the effects of these
635: disaster measure s with deaths per capita not statistically significant.
636: Data also suggest a negative association of repetition rates with variables like the parents ’ income,
637: the ratio of teachers to students, government spending per student and parents’ education. But we see a
638: statistically significant impact of government exp enditure per student such that a 1% increase in
641: that discusses the importance of an increase in parent and school resources on student performance
642: (Hanushek , 1997 ; Barro -Lee, 1993, 2001, 2010 ). Although household may form an inelastic demand for
643: education, the likelihood of a child to repeat a grade is more influenced by the e conomic damages from
644: disasters.
645: After controlling for country and year specific characteristics and socioeconomic determinants ,
646: we find that deaths per capita affect the stock of educa tional attainment at a 5% level of significance while
647: damages per capita influence the flows of educational attainment.
649: 5 Cava llo et al (2009) discuss the issue of poorer countries facing higher effects of disaster because they take on more
650: pro-cyclical fiscal policies rather than counter -cyclical policies that are relevant for reconstruction. Where advanced
651: countries adopt counter -cyclical fiscal policies, they increase spending and reduce taxes to adopt recuperative measures.
669: of disasters that lead to income loss and cuts in expenditure still stand as a challeng e for developing
670: countries. Developed countries have built stable mechanisms to reduce death effects and the after -effects
671: of disaster shocks but human capital is still at risk where we see still a wide range of underperforming
672: intervention policies and lo w commitment to education.
676: to secondary schooling atta inment poses a significant threat for secondary school attainment. It is also
677: important to note the significance of parents’ education in increasing educational attainment across all
678: specifications. This explains the current and contrasting educational att ainment across regions and is
679: consistent with the path -dependency theory. In cases where shocks have hindered attainment for children
680: in the 60s -90s, this may spill over to their own children in the 21st century (Baez et al, 2010). The l iterature
681: on repeti tion rates and likely external shocks that affect these rates has not been widely discussed but data
682: here shows some evidence that the intensity of damages increases repetition rates in secondary schools.
684: This study provides insight into crucial factors t hat create gaps in education al levels for countries
685: that are well -prepared to handle disasters and countries that are not. This informs the need for country -
686: specific coping and risk management strategies to tailor plans for countries to prevent degradation of
687: elements of economic devel opment. It is also relevant in motivating policies that cater to the psychological
688: and behavioral responses of disadvantaged groups mostly in developing countries . Humanitarian efforts
689: and conditional transfer programs have been slowly rising but governmen ts have to ensure that the right
694: before, during and a fter a disaster (Gitter and Braham, 2006) . Research shows that higher levels of past
695: educational attainment determine the gravity of a disaster (Skidmore and Toya, 2006). Therefore, guarding
696: education for the present generation is indicative of how good or bad effects from disasters would be.
700: research in addi tion to exploring the impact of these shocks on the quality of education at all levels which
701: will strengthen country -specific policy interventions and place a stronger emphasis on the dangers
702: education faces during natural disaster events , especially for d eveloping countries.

32: IZA DP No. 1398
33: How Different Are Immigrants?
34: A Cross-Country and Cross-SurveyAnalysis of Educational Achievement
35: Sylke Viola SchnepfDISCUSSION PAPER SERIES
36: Forschungsinstitut
40: How Different Are Immigrants?
41: A Cross-Country and Cross-Survey
42: Analysis of Educational Achievement
112: How Different Are Immigrants? A Cross-Country and
113: Cross-Survey Analysis of Educational Achievement
115: This paper examines differences in educational achievement between immigrants and
116: natives in ten countries with a high population of immigrant pupils: Australia, Canada, France, Germany, the Netherlands, New Zealand, Sweden, Switzerland, the UK and the USA. The first step of the analysis shows how far countries differ regarding immigrants’ educational disadvantage. In a second step, the paper compares immigrants’ characteristics across countries focusing predominantly on socioeconomic status, language proficiency, immigrants’
117: time spent in the host country and patterns of school segregation. Using a regression
118: framework the last step of the analysis investigates how far these determinants of educational achievement can explain immigrant s’ educational disadvantage in the countries
119: examined. The paper evaluates whether results found are robust across different sources of achievement data: the Trends in International Maths and Science Study (TIMSS), the
120: Programme of International Student Assessment (PISA) and the Programme of International
121: Reading Literacy Study (PIRLS).
127: Keywords: education, immigration, PISA, TIMSS, PIRLS
141: 1 Introduction
142: In many OECD countries migration plays an important and growing role in
143: contributing to the population growth. The education of immigrants is a determining
144: factor for their integration into the labour market and the society. The first aim of this
145: paper is to analyse how immigrants differ from natives regarding educational
146: outcomes and its determinants. We focus on ten immigration countries where the
147: share of the foreign born in the total population is similar or greater than 10 percent1:
148: Australia, Canada, France, Germany, the Netherlands, New Zealand, Sweden,
149: Switzerland, the UK and the USA.
150: Literature examining educational disadvantages of immigrants focus generally
151: on data on educational attainment that captures progression up national educational
152: systems (e.g. Ours and Veenman 2001, Riphahn 2002). The aim of this paper is to
153: examine educational achievement that refers to educational outcomes like ability or
154: ‘functional literacy’ (the ability to function in modern society). Educational
155: achievement can be compared more easily across countries than educational
156: attainment that partly reflects countries’ institutional differences in how education is
157: organised. In addition, a specific degree used as a measure of educational attainment
158: might mirror different ability for immigrants and natives even in the same country. In
159: general, immigrants have a lower informational access on their host countries
160: educational system. Hence, their chances to reach a specific educational attainment
161: are not only dependent on their general ability but also on their integration into the
162: host country. The focus on educational achievement overcomes these problems and is
163: only seldom applied in the literature2.
164: For examining educational achievement we can make use of recent
165: international surveys of learning achievement of children in compulsory school. These
166: surveys focus on what pupils actually know or can do. But which achievement survey
167: to use? Each survey aims to assess something different (e.g. maths or reading
168: achievement) or to assess knowledge in a different way (e.g. in relation to an
170: 1 See OECD 2003 and Table A1 in the appendix.
171: 2 One very recent exemption is Entorf and Minoiu (2004). This paper uses exclusively PISA data for
172: comparing achievement differences between immigrants and natives in 8 high and one low
173: immigration country. Besides the approach of testing the robustness of survey results by using PISA,
174: TIMSS and PIRLS data our paper emphasise the transparency of determinants of educational
175: achievement used and explains the extent of immigrants disadvantage in terms of something readily
177: - 3 -
179: and uses different methods for assessment (more open-ended or multiple-choice
180: questions). Due to immigrants’ limited language skills and different cultural
181: background we might expect them to fare better in the subject maths than reading, in a
182: more “curriculum based” approach measure of ability and with non-wordy multiple-
183: choice questions. Hence, the variations between surveys are very likely to impact
184: upon educational achievement results for immigrants. Our second aim in this paper is
185: to pull together the evidence from three different surveys to see if a robust picture
186: exists on immigrants’ educational disadvantage in the ten high immigration countries.
187: There are several factors that impact upon differences in educational
188: achievement between immigrants and natives. These factors motivate the research
189: interest of this paper but also show its limitations.
190: First, educational achievement per se is determined greatly by pupils’ socio-
191: economic background. For this reason, differences between immigrants and natives
192: regarding their family background can be a determining factor for achievement
193: differences between both groups of children. Hence, a main research interest of this
194: paper regards the question in how far the different composition of immigrants and
195: natives is related to their differences in educational achievement. Nevertheless, SES is
196: partly unobservable, especially if we focus on parental education that is measured by
197: educational attainment. An immigrant’s mother who has completed upper secondary
198: education in Mexico and a native’s mother who holds an upper secondary degree
199: achieved in the USA have probably a quite different quality of education. Hence, the
201: education is similar. We use also another more easily comparable measure of SES
202: across countries – books at home -, nevertheless, up to a certain degree we cannot
203: capture the “real” amount of SES differences between immigrant and native pupils.
204: Second, educational achievement differences can also derive from immigrants’
205: problems of integration into the host country. On one hand, the ability of the
206: immigrant pupil to communicate in the language of the host country is a crucial factor
207: for educational achievement. Hence, we estimate the impact of a foreign language
208: spoken at home on educational achievement. In addition, by focusing on different
210: understood. In addition, we focus on immigrants' educational achievement dispersion and examine also
211: the impact on the school level by focusing on school segregation.
213: - 4 -
214: subjects it will also be examined whether the immigrants’ achievement gap is smaller
215: for more technical subjects requiring less language skills like maths.
216: On the other hand, the problem of integration into the host society is also
218: However, the difficulty of integrating into a host society is a function of the
219: time pupils are living in the host country. We will therefore focus not only on
220: immigrants defined as pupils whose both parents were born abroad but also give some
221: achievement results on two different kinds of immigrants: a) non-native immigrant
222: pupils who were born abroad and b) first-generation immigrant pupils who were born
223: in the host countries.
224: Nevertheless, integrating into the host society might also be complicated by
225: residential and school segregation. A high clustering of ethnicities in neighbourhoods
227: than in a ‘mixed’ environment. In addition, a high school segregation (that is often
228: related to high residential segregation) might have some impact on immigrants’
229: educational achievement. Pupils are likely to be influenced by their peers’ school
230: ambitions and these are likely to be different in highly segregated schools. This
231: provides the motivation for this paper to estimate additionally the impact of school
232: segregation on educational achievement gaps between immigrants and natives.
233: Third, even if socio-economic background between immigrants and natives
234: were equal and immigrants were integrated successfully into the society, the process
235: of selection impacting upon the characteristics of migrants living in a host country
236: might still mould achievement differences between natives and immigrants. On one
237: hand, a selection through immigration control takes place. In traditional countries of
238: immigration like Australia, New Zealand and Canada immigration policies try to
239: attract highly skilled immigrants (Inglis 2002, Ray 2002, Bedford 2003). These
240: immigrants are very likely to differ in their motivations and expectations from
241: immigrants in the former ‘guest worker’ countries like Switzerland and Germany
242: (Castles and Miller 2003). Partly, it is possible to take these selection effects into
243: account by focusing on the before discussed issue of socio-economic background.
244: However, besides SES also motivations and expectations of immigrants on their life
245: in the host country are very much likely to impact upon their offsprings’ educational
246: achievement. Hence, the selection issue remains partly an unobservable that cannot be
247: properly addressed by comparing educational achievement gaps between immigrants
249: - 5 -
250: and natives. This is also true for the self-selection of immigrants. The self-selection of
251: immigrants captures that fact, that immigrants might have very different
252: characteristics depending on the host country they decide to live in and their country
253: of origin. Turkish immigrants deciding to live in Canada might be much different
254: from Turkish immigrants living in Germany. Asian immigrants differ greatly from
255: Mexican immigrants in the US (Schmid 2001, Glick and White 2003). A focus on
256: educational dispersion between immigrants can give an impression how different
257: immigrants' educational achievement is in the high immigration countries. Hence, we
258: will compare the achievement distributions of immigrants with those of natives.
259: However, the focus on immigrants' dispersion can only indicate the very different
260: nature of immigrants in one country but cannot capture the effect of selection that is
261: likely to determine achievement differences between immigrants and natives
262: differently in diverse countries.
263: The paper is structured as follows. Section 2 introduces the three surveys we
264: draw on: the Trends in International Maths and Science Study (TIMSS), the
265: Programme of International Student Assessment (PISA) and the Programme of
266: International Reading Literacy Study (PIRLS). Section 3 examines how different
267: immigrants are regarding their educational achievement and compares educational
268: dispersion between immigrants and natives across countries and surveys. Section 4
269: discusses the differences in socio-economic background between immigrants and
270: natives and examines school segregation in the ten high immigration countries.
271: However, are countries differences in immigrants' educational disadvantage a
272: mere function of immigrants' differences in socio-economic background compared to
273: natives? Can school segregation explain the immigrants' achievement gap found in
274: some countries? Section 5 uses a regression framework for examining these questions
275: and compares regression results across surveys and countries. Section 6 concludes.
277: 2 Data
278: Table 1 lists the educational achievement surveys3 and their coverage of ten countries
279: selected for this analysis due to their high share of immigrant pupils and their
280: participation in at least two of the surveys. All three data sources relate to children in
281: compulsory schooling and are recent pertaining to 1995, 1999, 2000 and 2001. While
283: 3 Details on the surveys can be found in their reports: Mullis et al (2000), Mullis et al (2003), OECD
284: and Statistics Canada (2000) and OECD (2001).
286: - 6 -
287: PIRLS focused on primary school children aged 9-10 years4, TIMSS and PISA
288: covered children in secondary school. PISA data focused on pupils who are 15 years
289: old, TIMSS5 covered 7th and 8th graders. The surveys' sample designs involve the
290: selection of a sample of schools and then a single class (TIMSS and PIRLS) or a
291: random sample (PISA) of pupils within each school.
292: The three surveys differ considerably so that the choice of the survey for
293: examining natives' and immigrants' achievement might impact upon the results.
294: Different surveys assessed different types of achievement, covered different subjects
295: and collected information differently. PISA assessed ability in reading, science and
296: maths with the aim to measure broad skills, trying to look at how students would be
297: able to use what they have learned in real-life situations. PIRLS measured primary
298: school children's reading and understanding capability of written texts. In contrast to
299: PISA and PIRLS, TIMSS focused on assessing a mastery of internationally agreed
300: curricula in the subjects maths and science.
301: PISA assessed achievement predominantly by using open-ended questions. In
302: contrary, about two-thirds of the TIMSS questions were multiple choice in 1999.
303: Since on average immigrants have lower language skills than their native
304: counterparts, achievement gaps are likely to be lower between native and immigrant
305: pupils with the ability measure of TIMSS that uses multiple choice questions on
306: technical curriculum based subjects like maths and science.
307: In addition, surveys differ regarding their application of aggregation methods
308: for transferring results into the final analysed achievement measure. Survey organisers
309: do not report the sensitivity of results to the choice of model but Brown and
310: Micklewright (2003) show with TIMSS data that this is not a trivial issue.
311: Other differences can be cited, including response rates. Even the basic
312: premise that culturally-neutral questions can be successfully designed and translated
315: 4 PIRLS assessed children in the upper of the two grades with the most 9-year–olds at the time of
316: testing. This corresponds to the fourth grade and an average age of about 10 years for most of the
317: countries.
318: 5 TIMSS 1995 covered 3rd and 4th grades, 7th and 8th grades and the last grade of secondary schooling.
319: TIMSS 1999 assessed children in the 8th grade only. We focus on 8th grade data for 1995 for all
320: countries except for USA where most recent data is available for all variables used in the regression
321: analysis. (England, New Zealand, Canada, Australia and Netherlands did participate in 1999 but did not
322: administer all data on pupils family background in this year. In order to be consistent with data used the
323: analysis refers always to 1995 data for these countries.) When using 1995 data we used ‘rescaled’ data
324: – scores derived from a psychometric model of the same form as that used for the 1999 data.
326: - 7 -
327: varying from survey to survey.6 In short, there seems ample reason for comparing
328: results across the different surveys rather than relying on a single source.

344: Note: TIMSS and PIRLS are organised by the International Study Center, Boston College, USA. PISA is
345: organised by OECD. France is covered by TIMSS in 1995, but the variable used for the calculation of
346: pupils' immigration status was not administered. Data on Germany, Sweden and Switzerland refer to 1995
347: for TIMSS; in the regression analysis only data of the USA refer to 1999 (for details see appendix). Data
348: for UK in TIMSS and PIRLS refer to England and Scotland only. Data on Netherlands for PISA might be
349: biased due to very low response rate. Nevertheless, we decided to use this data, since it is possible to
350: compare PISA results with results of the other two surveys.
352: Table 1 shows that PISA covered all ten countries with a high immigration
353: background, while Australia and Switzerland did not participate in PIRLS. TIMSS
354: covered all ten countries, but the variable used for the calculation of pupils'
355: immigration status was not administered in France.
356: In all three surveys information were collected in the same format regarding
357: two immigration variables: First, whether pupils, their mothers and fathers were born
358: in the test country or abroad and second, how often the language of the test country is
359: spoken at home. For the purpose of this paper immigrants are defined as pupils whose
360: both parents were born abroad. First generation immigrants were born in the test
361: country while non-native immigrants where born abroad. Children who are not
362: immigrants, hence who have at least one parent born in the test country, are referred
363: to as natives. Table A1 in the appendix presents the share of the immigrants in the
364: surveys' sample and the foreign born population expressed as share of the countries'
365: population based on OECD data. In general, estimates of percentages of pupils with
367: 6 Overall country response rates in TIMSS and PISA averaged 88 percent (after replacement of non-
368: responding schools with substitutes) and 85 percent respectively. Response in PIRLS averaged 92
369: percent (unweighted) and in IALS 62 percent. Variation across countries can be marked. Blum et al
370: (2001) consider France's experience in IALS (a survey on adults literacy) and among other things
371: make critical comparison of the French language questionnaire used in France and that used in
372: Switzerland. (France originally participated and then later withdrew.)
374: - 8 -
375: immigration background are relatively similar between surveys. An exception is the
376: USA with a share of immigrant pupils of 10 percent for TIMSS and a very high
377: percentage of 20 for PIRLS.
379: 3 Educational achievement gaps between immigrants and natives
380: We start the discussion of achievement gaps between immigrants and natives by
381: focusing on just one subject and survey, maths achievement in TIMSS. Figure 1
382: presents the percentage of natives (x-axis) and immigrants (y-axis) who do not reach
383: the international TIMSS maths median. These children are viewed as `unable to apply
384: basic mathematic knowledge in straightforward situations' by the organisers.
385: (Obviously these classifications are open to debate.) For each country two values are
386: given on the y-axis, one value for first-generation immigrants (born in the test
387: country, indicated by the square) and one value for non-native immigrants (born
388: abroad, indicated by diamond). Immigrants in Canada, Australia, the UK and non-
389: native immigrants in New Zealand are situated around the 45 degree line showing that
390: the percentage of immigrants and natives achieving below TIMSS math international
391: median are similar in these countries. For the other countries a higher percentage of
392: immigrants than natives are unable to solve basic maths tasks. In Switzerland,
393: Sweden, Germany and the USA the share of non-native immigrants lacking basic
394: maths skills is significantly higher than that of first-generation immigrants, indicating
395: that pupils grown up in the host country are achieving better than newly arrived
396: immigrant pupils. As argued before, a higher integration into the host country due to
397: longer time spent in that country is likely to impact positively on achievement score.
398: However, in Netherlands and New Zealand we find the reverse effect, with a lower
399: share of newly arrived immigrants than those born in the country who cannot solve
400: basic mathematic tasks (PISA maths achievement scores show the same counter-
401: intuitive result only for New Zealand). This result for Netherlands and New Zealand
402: might be explained by the much higher level of parental education for non-native than
403: for first-generation immigrants7.
406: 7 Given TIMSS data, 46 percent of the parents of first-generation immigrants in New Zealand have
407: completed secondary education compared to 65 percent of parents of non-natives. The trend is smaller
408: in the Netherlands with 21 percent of first-generation and 29 percent of non-native immigrants' parents
409: who completed secondary schooling. Similar results for Netherlands are found by van Ours and
410: Veenman (2000).However, also in Australia and Germany the parental education of non-natives is on
411: average higher than that of first-generation immigrants.
413: - 9 -
414: Figure 1: Percentage of natives and immigrants (first-generation immigrants and non-native
415: immigrants) achieving below the international TIMSS median
416: USA
417: GBRCHE SWENZL

428: Note: Immigrant pupils are pupils whose both parents were born in a foreign country. Non-native
429: immigrant pupils were born abroad and first-generation immigrants were born in the test country.
430: Native pupils refer to all other children.
432: However, the most surprising result of Figure 1 is the great difference in
433: percentages of natives and immigrants not able to solve basic maths tasks in some of
434: the countries examined. In Germany and Sweden more than 20 percent and in
435: Switzerland more than 30 percent of non-native immigrants than of natives fall below
436: the international median of TIMSS maths achievement. Expressed differently, one
437: and a half times more non-native immigrants than natives in the US, Germany and
438: Netherlands, about two times more in Sweden and three times more in Switzerland
439: are unable to solve basic maths tasks. On the other hands, the percentage of low
440: achieving immigrants is very similar to that of natives in the English-speaking
441: countries Canada, Australia and the UK.
442: In PISA, children who score beneath a critical benchmark level of competence
443: (the reading literacy level 2) are viewed as `unable to solve basic reading tasks such as
445: - 10 -
446: locating straightforward information'. Applying this survey organisers' benchmark to
447: pupils with different migration background (without differentiation between non-
448: natives and first-generation immigrants) shows that the group of immigrants unable to
449: solve basic reading tasks is about 10 percent higher than the groups of natives in the
450: UK, France and USA, 20 percent higher in the Netherlands, Switzerland and Sweden
451: and even 30 percent higher in Germany. In line with TIMSS results, the percentage of
452: low ability pupils is similar for natives and immigrants in Canada, Australia and New
453: Zealand.
454: Up to now we compared percentages of immigrant and native pupils falling
455: below an absolute educational benchmark. The advantage of this approach is that it is
456: possible to attribute a meaning in form of a statement about ability to students
458: two groups: those above and those below the international benchmark.8 A more
459: common way for reporting educational achievement results is to report the countries'
460: average survey scores. These scores lack a natural metric and are therefore difficult to
461: interpret. In general, countries covered in the surveys9 have a mean score set to 500
462: and a standard deviation of 100.
463: Figure 2 presents average achievement scores for all immigrants (x-axis) and
464: natives (y-axis) for PISA reading. With the exception of Ireland and Hungary all
465: countries are situated above the 45 degree line indicating that natives fare better in
466: reading achievement than immigrants.
467: The general picture reveals that the selected ten high immigration countries are
468: evenly distributed among other OECD countries and hence do not differ greatly from
469: them regarding achievement gaps between pupils with different immigration
470: background. The small correlation coefficient between achievement of natives and the
471: achievement gaps between immigrants and natives (the difference between natives'
472: and immigrants' mean achievement) of 0.18 for all OECD countries covered indicates
473: that countries with high average educational achievement (e.g. Finland and
475: 8 Another disadvantage regarding the focus on absolute educational disadvantage regards the general
476: country level of achievement. Differences between immigrants and natives in a country can be large
477: but do not translate into great differences between shares of low ability immigrants and nativesin case
478: the countries' mean achievement is very high.
479: 9 The problem for comparing different survey scores is that each survey covers a different set of
480: countries. For example, the mean score of 500 applies only to OECD countries for PISA but to all
481: countries covered by PIRLS (including e.g. Kuwait and Iran).
483: - 11 -
484: Netherlands) are not necessarily successful in limiting immigrants' educational
485: disadvantage.

504: Differences between the high immigration countries are large. There does not
505: seem to be a big achievement gap between the two groups of pupils in English-
506: speaking countries in general; especially in Australia and Canada immigrants are not
507: greatly different from natives regarding their educational achievement. On the other
508: hand, point differences are considerably high for pupils in Germany, Switzerland and
509: the Netherlands where immigrants received an average achievement score 75 points
510: below that of natives. As discussed before, for Germany this is similar to 30 percent
511: more immigrants than natives who are not able to solve basic reading tasks. Sweden,
512: France and the US are in a middle position given that immigrants show an about 40 to
513: 60 point lower average achievement than natives.
514: Is this result on country differences in immigrants' educational achievement
515: gap robust if we focus on other subjects and surveys?

541: TIMSS data can give us a reasonable tool at hand for interpreting differences
542: in mean achievement scores between immigrants and natives. TIMSS tested 7th and
543: 8th graders in 1995. On average11, 8th graders show an about 30 point higher
544: achievement in TIMSS maths and fare 40 points better in TIMSS science than 7th
545: graders. Expressing the native-immigrant achievement gap in years of grade
546: progression shows how many years of schooling immigrants lack compared to
547: natives.
548: In almost all countries and surveys immigrants achieve significantly lower test
549: scores than natives. In addition, survey and subject results show a relatively consistent
550: ranking of countries on achievement differences between immigrants and natives.
551: Switzerland, Germany and Netherlands appear to have greatest differences between
552: both groups of pupils throughout most of the surveys' measures. In these three
553: countries TIMSS results show that pupils whose parents were born abroad lack about
555: 10 With more recent TIMSS data for 1999 immigrants educational disadvantage is higher for the
556: Netherlands (-37 points for maths and -65 points for science) and smaller for Canada (-3 points for
557: maths and -26 points for science) and New Zealand (+ 6 points for maths and ±21 for science). These
558: results comply more with PISA results.

560: average country differences between mean upper and mean lower grade achievement. However, there
561: are notable country differences in grade progression. E.g. in Netherlands and Germany 8th graders
562: perform only about 20 points better than 7th graders, while pupils in the upper grade in Sweden perform
563: about 37 points better than their lower grade counterparts.
566: one year of schooling compared to natives in maths and almost two in science.
567: Immigrants in Sweden fare better regarding their educational disadvantage in PISA
568: than these three countries, but similarly bad in TIMSS and PIRLS. The second
569: country group contains France, the USA and the UK, with a still moderate
570: achievement difference between immigrants and natives. The last group of countries,
571: Canada, Australia and New Zealand, consists of an immigrant population that faces
572: only low or no significant educational disadvantages compared to the native
573: population.
574: Also the correlation coefficients between all surveys given in Table A2 in the
575: appendix indicate the high agreement between surveys on immigrants disadvantage in
576: countries. The lowest correlation coefficient between surveys is for achievement gaps
577: in PIRLS reading and TIMSS science with 0.6712.
578: We might expect that immigrants' educational disadvantage is smaller in
579: subjects where language skills are generally of a lower importance like in maths.13
580: However, achievement in maths in PISA is measured by applying the `life-skill'
581: approach related to open-ended questions on wordy descriptions of `real life'
582: situations. Hence, it is not necessarily surprising, that immigrants do not fare better in
583: maths in PISA. This result stands in contrast to TIMSS: for all countries immigrants'
584: educational achievement gap is significantly lower in TIMSS maths than in TIMSS
585: science. Hence, there seems to be the tendency that immigrants' educational
586: disadvantage is smaller in technical subjects as long as achievement is assessed in a
587: more curriculum based approach by using predominantly multiple-choice questions
588: (like done in TIMSS).
589: Given that, we might also expect that in subjects that require mainly language
590: skills newly arrived non-native immigrants show lower educational achievement than
591: first-generation immigrants. Table 3 splits average achievement differences between
592: natives and immigrants given in Table 2 for PISA and PIRLS reading into average
593: achievement differences between natives and non-native immigrants and between
594: natives and first-generation immigrants.

598: between surveys is 0.8 (PISA science with PIRLS reading) indicating the even greater agreement
599: between surveys once the most recent data are used.

629: gap is considerably higher for non-native immigrants than for first-generation

633: Until now the discussion of immigrants' educational disadvantage treated
634: pupils with parents born abroad as a more or less homogenous group. However, there
635: is some evidence, that immigrants are not almost worse performers as average
636: calculations applied before imply for some countries. Immigrant pupils can differ
637: greatly depending on their parents' country of origin, ethnicity and socio-economic
638: background. Some literature show that Asian pupils outperform natives in the US
639: (Glick and White 2003) and Chinese and Indian pupils are better achievers than their
640: English counterparts (Demie 2001). Even though the survey data do not allow for this
641: specification, it is possible to examine differences between immigrants by
642: investigating the variation in their achievement. A focus on immigrants' achievement
643: distribution compared to natives can also shed light on where achievement differences
644: between natives and immigrants derive from in different countries. Are immigrants
646: 13 Given that survey organisers claim their assessment measure not to be culturally biased, we might
647: assume that achievement differences between immigrants and natives do not derive from
649: - 15 -
650: always worse performers than natives? Or is there only a greater share of badly
651: performing immigrants than natives that leads to an on average lower performance of
652: immigrants? In all ten countries for PISA reading and in seven countries (of nine) for
653: TIMSS maths immigrants’ dispersion is higher than that of natives (see Table A3 in
654: the appendix giving the difference between the 95th and 5th percentile in achievement
655: for immigrants and natives separately). This suggests that immigrants’ achievement
656: distribution is not similar to that of natives and not only shifted into the direction of
657: lower achievement.
658: In order to compare the achievement distribution of immigrants and natives we
659: estimate the achievement points for the 5th, 10th, 15th,… and 95th percentile for both
660: groups of children separately for the ten countries (achievement scores by percentile
661: and migration background are reported in Table A4 in the appendix). Then we
662: calculate the ratio of the achievement scores (immigrants’ score divided by natives’
663: score) for each percentile and country. The results are presented in Figure 3.
664: Throughout all countries and percentiles the ratio is smaller than 1 indicating
665: that immigrants’ achievement is in all percentiles worse than that of natives. In
666: addition, for all countries immigrants’ achievement disadvantage is always more
667: pronounced at the bottom than at the top of the two distributions. However, countries
668: differ regarding the intercept and the slope of the achievement ratio.
669: Not surprisingly, countries where immigrants’ educational achievement is
670: similar to that of natives (Australia, Canada and New Zealand) appear to have an
671: achievement ratio closer to 1 across all percentiles than countries with greater average
672: achievement gaps like Netherlands, Germany and Switzerland (see country ranking in
673: Table 2). In Australia and Canada, immigrants at the bottom of the achievement
674: distribution (5th percentile) show an only about 5 percent lower reading achievement
675: than natives in the same percentile. On the other hand, in Switzerland, Germany and
676: Netherlands immigrants at the bottom of the distribution have achievement scores that
677: are about 25 percent lower than that of their native counterparts situated in the 5th
678: percentile.

700: There are also great differences at the top of the distribution. Best performing
701: immigrants achieve similarly to best ranked natives in Australia, Canada, New
702: Zealand, the UK and the USA. Hence, in these countries there is a relative small
703: achievement gap between children with different migration background once the
704: distribution of achievement at the top is concerned. This stands in contrast to the three
705: countries Netherlands, Germany and Switzerland, where even the best achieving
706: immigrants reach scores that are still about 15 percent lower than that of natives at the
707: top of the distribution.
708: Notable is the steep rise in achievement differences between natives and
709: immigrants once achievement at the bottom scale is concerned in New Zealand, the
710: US and UK. While in these countries immigrants in the 95th percentile do not differ
711: greatly from their native counterparts, pupils with parents born abroad show almost a
712: 15 percent lower achievement than natives once we focus on achievement ratios at the
714: marginally for increasing percentiles in France.
715: Taken together, surveys show relative consistent results on educational
716: achievement gaps between immigrants and natives. In traditional countries of
718: - 17 -
719: immigration like Australia, Canada and New Zealand immigrants do not appear to be
720: greatly different from natives. On the other hand, in European countries like the
721: Netherlands, Germany and Switzerland immigrants fare significantly worse than
722: natives. These average achievement differences are great in magnitude as
723: comparisons to grade progression achievement differences and absolute disadvantage
724: showed.
725: Educational dispersion between immigrants is in general even higher than that
726: between natives. PISA results show that in countries where immigrants' educational
727: achievement is similar to that of natives' immigrants at the bottom of the achievement
728: distribution show only slightly worse performance than their native counterparts. In
729: countries with great immigrants' educational disadvantage achievement differences
730: between immigrants and natives are large especially at the bottom of the achievement
731: distribution. 15
732: The next section aims at throwing light on the determinants of immigrants'
733: educational disadvantage.
735: 4 Determinants of immigrants’ achievement
736: Educational achievement is greatly related to pupils' socio-economic background.
737: Sub-section 4.1 discusses the different socio-economic background between natives
738: and immigrants that certainly is part of the explanation of immigrants' achievement
739: gaps. However, pupils integration into the `school environment' is also likely to
740: matter. For countries with great immigrants' educational disadvantage we might
741: assume that immigrants who are highly separated from natives in school perform
742: generally worse than immigrants who are integrated into the host countries' school
743: environment more equally. Sub-section 4.2 discusses the relation between school
744: segregation and immigrants' performance.
745: Sub-section 4.3 applies regression analysis in order to analyse whether
746: immigrants still face educational disadvantage in the ten countries once it is controlled
747: for pupils' characteristics like SES and school segregation.
750: 14 This explains that immigrants' dispersion in these countries is much greater than that of natives.
751: 15 The most extreme case is the Netherlands where immigrants in the 5th percentile achieve PISA
752: reading scores that are similar to that of natives in the 75th percentile (see Table A3 in the appendix).
753: However, response rate in the Netherlands was very low so that PISA results for this country need to be
754: interpreted carefully.
756: - 18 -
757: 4.1 Differences in socio-economic background between natives and immigrants
758: There are a number of variables that could be used to investigate the association
759: between socio-economic background and the native-immigrant achievement gap. The
760: main PISA report (OECD 2001) placed considerable emphasis on the association of
761: scores with indices constructed from principal components analysis of a range of
762: parental characteristics, including occupational indices based on the work of
763: Ganzeboom et al (1992). Obviously these indices cannot be replicated for other
764: surveys that have collected different family background data and we take a much
765: simpler approach in order to make comparisons between the different data sources.16
766: This also has the merit of greater transparency with variables that are relatively easy
769: estimation of the number of books at home. This measure of SES has the great
770: advantage of being comparable across countries and immigrant populations (in
771: contrast to migrants' parental education17). We might assume that children's estimates
772: of books at home are quite unreliable, but the correlation coefficient of 0.93 between
773: the percentage of pupils with more than 100 books at home for PISA and the same
774: share of children for TIMSS shows a great agreement between both surveys.
775: A straightforward way for estimating socio-economic background differences
776: between immigrants and natives is to calculate the percentage difference between
777: natives and immigrants with more than 100 books at home. Figure 4 gives this
778: percentage difference on the x-axis.18 Obviously, differences between natives and
779: immigrants vary greatly across countries. In Canada and New Zealand only about 10
780: percent more natives than immigrants have more than 100 books at home, while the
781: figure rises to more than 35 percent in Sweden and the Netherlands and is similar to
782: 30 percent in Switzerland and Germany. This pattern reminds of the country ranking
783: on immigrants' educational disadvantage (Table 2) and indeed, once paired with
784: average mean achievement differences between natives and immigrants (y-axis) there
785: seems to be a quite great relationship. Higher SES differences between natives and
786: immigrants are positively related to higher immigrants' achievement gap with a
788: 16 Nevertheless we use the `International Socio-Economic Index of Occupational Status' (ISEI) for
789: estimating the pupils' composition in school in the later regression framework (Model 3) ± see Section
790: 4.3.
791: 17 See introduction for limitations of our estimation of immigrants' SES.
792: 18 For figures see Table A4 in the appendix.
794: - 19 -
795: correlation coefficient of 0.62 for all OECD countries and 0.82 for the ten high
796: immigration countries. This result suggests that differences between countries
797: regarding educational achievement of immigrants can be partly explained by the
798: different composition of immigrants in these countries.
799: Figure 4 presents results only for PISA reading, however other surveys'
800: outcomes are similar: the lower share of immigrants with more than 100 books at
801: home is correlated positively with greater immigrants' achievement differences
802: compared to natives in TIMSS math with 0.85, in TIMSS science with 0.82 and in
803: PIRLS reading with 0.70 for the ten high immigration countries.

806: immigrants in PISA reading

827: Table A4 in the appendix.

830: consider since it is closely related to children's learning achievement. However, as
831: discussed before the same level of education but achieved in the host country and in
832: the immigrants' country of origin might refer to quite different educational quality.
