859: maths, 0.67 for PISA reading, 0.65 for PIRLS and 0.62 for TIMSS science.
860: Taken together, those countries with immigrants' highest educational
861: disadvantage have a migrant composition with highest disadvantage in socio-
862: economic status. Does this mean that countries' differences in immigrants'
863: achievement gaps are a mere reflection of migrants' socio-economic status in these
864: countries? Or does immigrant status matter beyond SES? The regression framework
865: in sub-section 4.3 examines whether, in which countries and to what extent
866: immigrants' achievement gaps can be explained by countries' socio-economic gap
867: between natives and migrants.
869: 4.2 School segregation
870: Educational achievement differences between immigrants and natives might also
871: derive from immigrants' problems to integrate into the host country. Immigrants are
872: not only different from natives regarding their socio-economic background but also in
873: terms of their cultural attitudes, social contacts, their schooling ambitions, career
876: also `ethnic capital' matters as a whole set of ethnic characteristics to that children in
877: particular ethnic groups are exposed to. Even though immigrants belong to different
878: ethnicities, as a group they might share a similar `immigrant capital' that is not
879: uniform per se but ± at least in some countries - uniform different from that of natives.
880: If we apply furthermore Borjas argumentation on immigrants' school achievement it
881: is the exposure to this `immigrant capital' that is likely to impact upon children's
882: schooling outcome. The exposure might be transmitted by `neighbourhood' in terms
883: of residential segregation of ethnicities as found in Borjas (1995) or - closely related
884: to that - by school segregation.21 Here, the term `school segregation' refers to
885: differences in the distribution of immigrants and natives in schools and is
886: conceptually related to the impact of peers' achievement and learning attitudes on
887: general educational outcome at the school level. An immigrant child in a highly
889: 20 For PISA reading pupils' shares are given in Table A5 in the appendix.
890: 21 Residential segregation often largely determines school segregation as demonstrated in the case of
891: the US (Rivkin 1994).
893: - 21 -
894: segregated school with a high percentage of low performing immigrants is very much
895: likely to be pulled to the average of immigrants' achievement, while the same child
896: (with the same socio-economic background) integrated in a not segregated school
897: with a high percentage of well achieving natives is less likely to fall behind in
898: educational achievement.
899: Hence, school segregation as a measure of conglomeration of immigrant peers
900: might be helpful in explaining immigrants' educational achievement gap. In addition,
901: it is an important factor since it shows how successful countries are in integrating
902: immigrant minorities and hence are opening the opportunities for immigrants to take
903: advantage of a fruitful school environment that is equal to that of their native
904: counterparts.
912: where i refers to the school and total to the country. IMi denotes the number of
913: immigrants in the school and IMtotal the number of immigrants in the country. NAi
914: means the number of natives in the school and NAtotal denotes the number of natives in
915: the country. N is the number of schools in the country.
916: The dissimilarity index ranges from 0 to 1. Its great advantage is that it can be
917: interpreted easily: the dissimilarity index gives the fraction of immigrant pupils that
918: need to be moved to different schools in order to make each school have the same
919: share of immigrants in the country.
920: However, the estimation of this index with the data available is not
921: unproblematic. First, the data should provide representative samples of data on pupils'
922: immigration status within each school. This criterion is met by PISA since a sample of
923: about 35 students is randomly drawn from the 15 year olds attending the school.
924: However, this contrasts the sample design of TIMSS and PIRLS. These surveys select
925: randomly a single whole class within each school. This is a procedure that is likely to i=1 N IMi NAi
926: IMtotal NAtotal
931: individual school level if, as is common, there is `setting' for the subjects that are
932: tested in the survey (i.e. children separated into separate classes according to their
933: ability levels). In this case, the ability levels of the selected class will be more
934: homogenous than among all children of that age in the school. Given the correlation
935: between ability and immigration status, the same can be expected to be true of the
936: latter. Given this, we must restrict the estimation of the segregation index to PISA
937: data.
938: Second, also with PISA data the estimation of the segregation index is
939: problematic due to the stochastic nature of the segregation index and the relative small
940: sample size of pupils (35) drawn in a PISA school. Given the absolute value of the
941: index, any sampling error (hence a proportion of immigrants in the pupils' sample of
942: one school that is different to the proportion of immigrants in the 15 year old school
943: population) in schools with no segregation will result in an upwards bias to the
944: estimator of DI (e.g. Ransom 2000, Cortese et al. 1976). However, the case of no
946: case of existing segregation, segregation indices distributions are more symmetric.
947: Nevertheless, given small sample sizes of schools and pupils in schools we cannot
948: reject that estimates might show an upward bias of the segregation index.
949: For the calculation of all national segregation index we need further bear in
950: mind that immigrant groups are not evenly spread over the countries so that the
951: national level of the index is quite superficial. The segregation of immigrants with
952: different countries of origin is also likely to vary greatly. Focusing on different ethnic
953: background Burgess and Wilson (2003) find for England dissimilarity indices (using
954: the same DI) that range from 0.448 for pupils with Chinese ethnic origin to 0.746 for
956: However, even though there is a need of some scepticism regarding the
957: appropriateness of the PISA data for the calculation of segregation index, school
958: segregation is likely to be an important explanatory variable for immigrants'
959: achievement gap at least at the individual level where problems of the calculation
960: discussed before impact less than regarding a comparison across countries.

974: percentage of immigrants in the 90th percentile of the school distribution indicates that
975: immigrants are highly clustered in a small number of schools. While it is difficult to
976: compare the distribution of the overall totals between countries, the segregation

1020: Even though the dissimilarity index comprises important limitations, the
1021: question arises whether the aggregate measure of school segregation is related to
1022: immigrants' educational achievement gaps. The correlation of the native-immigration
1023: gap in educational achievement for PISA reading (see Table 2) with the dissimilarity
1024: index (Table 4) results in a Pearson's correlation coefficient of -0.44 for DI. This
1025: indicates a relation in the opposite direction as assumed: on the aggregate country
1026: level countries with a greater segregation of immigrants in the school system appear
1027: to have a lower educational disadvantage of immigrant pupils.
1028: This result is not necessarily surprising taken into account that besides
1029: immigrants' integration into the country's school environment there are other factors
1030: impacting upon immigrants' educational disadvantage like parental socio-economic
1031: background. Once, these factors are hold constant school segregation might increase
1032: immigrants' disadvantage as we would expect. In addition, the focus on the
1033: aggregated country level is probably a much too crude measure for explaining
1034: immigrants' educational disadvantage.
1035: Hence, for the later regression analysis we estimate the schools' contribution
1036: to the (additive) country segregation index.

1043: theoretically range from ± 100 to + 100. A negative value indicates the share of
1044: natives that is overrepresented in the school compared to a school where immigrants
1045: and natives are distributed evenly based on their representation in the country. A
1046: positive value gives the overrepresentation of immigrants in the school.

1053: - 25 -
1054: The regression analysis in the next section uses this schools' contribution to
1055: the DI as an explanatory variable and shows whether immigrants in highly segregated
1056: schools face a greater educational disadvantage then their counterparts in other
1057: schools when other determinants of achievement at the individual and school level are
1060: 4.3 Regression analysis
1061: Does the socio-economic background gap between migrants and natives explain the
1062: country differences in immigrants' achievement gap? This question is of great
1063: relevance: in case immigrants' achievement differs to that of natives only due to their
1064: on average lower SES, educational policies would solely need to provide additional
1065: support for children with a disadvantaged family background. However, if immigrants
1066: face additional barriers besides SES, countries' educational policies need to adapt to
1067: immigrants' special needs in order to decrease their educational disadvantage.
1068: One great concern regarding educational policies might be school segregation
1069: as a measure of countries' capacity to integrate their immigrant population
1070: successfully into the schooling system. In how far is school segregation important for
1071: explaining educational achievement and in how far does it impact on immigrants'
1072: achievement?
1073: Table 5 investigates these issues and presents OLS regression results for three
1074: different models for PISA maths, TIMSS maths and PIRLS reading. The focus on
1075: maths has the advantage of standardising for subjects between the surveys with
1076: similarly old children in secondary school. In all regression models, children's
1077: achievement is the dependent variable. The table reports only the estimated effects of
1078: those explanatory variables related to immigration background. Native children are
1081: percent level, with one asterisk at the 5 and with two at the 10 percent level. Coding
1082: of variables used in the models, summary statistics of these variables by country and
1083: the whole regression results for Model 2 for all three surveys are given in the
1084: Appendix (Tables A6 ± A12).
1090: 3 by examining educational disadvantage for different types of migrant pupils. In the
1091: regression analysis we use three dummies on immigrant status: non-native
1092: immigrants, first-generation immigrants and pupils living in a home where the
1093: language spoken differs from the test country language. Coefficients of the three
1094: explanatory variables are presented in Model 1 of Table 5. The base pupil is native
1095: and speaks the test countries' language at home.
1096: Language spoken at home does not capture only immigrants but also other
1097: children from different ethnicities.25 For that reason, we ran the same regression
1098: model by additionally using an interaction variable capturing immigrants who speak
1099: another language at home. In general this variable was not significant26 so that we
1100: chose the more simple regression model presented here.
1101: In all surveys and countries children who speak a foreign language at home
1102: receive lower achievement scores than other children. This effect is consistently
1103: greatest in Germany with as many as 76 points in PISA and 44 points in TIMSS.
1104: However, foreign language appears to impact less on achievement in Canada, Sweden
1105: and France and only marginally in Netherlands, where the coefficients are
1106: insignificant (TIMSS) or below 20 points (PISA and PIRLS). To help interpret the
1107: coefficients, it is worth bearing in mind that the standard deviation in mean
1108: achievement is close to 100 for countries and that progression in average scores
1109: between 7th and 8th grades in TIMSS maths is on average 30 points.
1110: Once immigration status is concerned, the regression results show a great
1111: variation between countries as the unconditional analysis revealed before. In general,
1112: PISA and TIMSS results are quite consistent for secondary school pupils' maths
1113: achievement, while PIRLS results on primary school immigrants' reading
1114: achievement varies more once we compare countries' results between surveys.
1115: In PISA and TIMSS and similar to the presented unconditional results before
1116: immigration status does not have a significant negative impact on achievement in
1117: Australia and Canada. However, as unconditional results in Section 3 revealed,
1119: 25 For PISA maths in general more than 75 percent of children who speak another language at home
1120: than that in the test country are immigrants. However, in Canada and Netherlands the share is much
1121: smaller (58 % and 27 %).
1123: - 27 -
1124: immigrants face an educational disadvantage in the UK and the USA. Nevertheless,
1125: once language is controlled for, immigration does not matter any more in both
1126: countries (with the exception of non-natives in the USA in TIMSS). This indicates,
1127: that in the UK and USA language skills of immigrants might be the greatest barrier
1128: for immigrants to reach similar achievement than their native counterparts.
1129: A similar `effect' appears in Germany, where immigrants educational
1130: disadvantage was large in the before presented unconditional results. However, once
1131: language hold constant, first generation immigrants (for PISA and TIMSS) do not
1132: differ any more from natives regarding their achievement skills.27
1133: On the other hand, in countries like France, Switzerland, Sweden and the
1134: Netherlands with low impact of language spoken at home, first generation immigrants
1135: and non-native face still a significant educational disadvantage (similar to
1136: unconditional results). Throughout all surveys and compared to other countries,
1137: almost always both types of immigrant pupils face the greatest educational
1138: disadvantage in the Netherlands.
1139: What conclusions can we draw on differences between first-generation
1140: migrants' and non-natives' educational achievement gap? In general, controlled for
1141: language first-generation pupils outperform greatly non-native pupils. In only four of
1142: the 27 regression results presented in Model 1 non-natives performed better than first
1143: generation immigrants. Surprising is the result for New Zealand. Non-native
1144: immigrants in this country outperform even native students (once language is
1145: controlled for) and this result is consistent for TIMSS and PISA.
1146: Model 2
1147: Sub-section 4.1 showed that there is a great correlation between immigrants'
1148: educational disadvantage and the socio-economic gap they face on the country level.
1149: Based on the pupils' level Model 2 aims at answering the question whether the socio-
1150: economic gap between immigrants and natives can explain country differences in
1151: immigrants educational achievement or whether immigrant status still matters once
1152: socio-economic background is controlled for. This is especially interesting for
1154: 26 However, in countries where the interaction variable turned out to be significant the value was
1155: positive, indicating that immigrants fare better than other children who do not speak the test country's
1156: language at home.
1157: 27 Based on educational attainment data and focusing on second generation immigrants Riphahn (2002)
1158: receives a contradictory result.
1160: - 28 -
1161: countries where immigrants educational disadvantage remained even if it was
1162: controlled for language spoken at home (Model 1).
1163: For answering this question a mix of individual and family characteristics are
1168: the pupils and the area of the school (rural or urban) is controlled for. Table A6 in the
1169: Appendix summarises the explanatory variables and their coding used in the
1170: regression framework. The base group child in Model 2 is a male native pupil, who
1171: speaks the test countries language at home, whose mother has not completed (upper)
1172: secondary education and who lives in a home with less than 100 books in an urban
1173: area. However, results in Table 5 present only the significant coefficients on the three
1174: immigration variables. (See Tables A11 to A13 in the appendix on full results.)
1175: Not surprisingly, regression coefficients for Australia, Canada and New
1176: Zealand ± the three countries with no or only marginal educational achievement gaps
1177: for migrants ±do not differ greatly from unconditional results or those presented in
1178: Model 1 once it is controlled for socio-economic status and family structure. Since
1179: immigrants background is similar to that of natives (see sub-section 3.1), also
1180: immigrants' achievement is still similar to that of natives in these countries once
1181: controlled for parental background, area and family structure.
1182: However, in the UK and US there appears the slight effect that once SES is
1183: controlled for immigrants fare even better than natives. However, the positive
1184: coefficients are always smaller than the coefficient for `language spoken at home' so
1185: that only immigrant pupils who speak the test language at home have a marginally
1186: higher educational advantage compared to their native counterparts in both countries.
1187: The most interesting results regard countries where immigrants were still
1188: different from natives in Model 1. In France, Netherlands, Sweden and Switzerland
1189: the impact of immigrant status shrinks greatly once SES is controlled for.
1190: Nevertheless, in all four countries in PISA first generation immigrants as well as non-
1191: natives fare still significantly worse in achievement than their native counterparts with
1192: similar SES and who speak the same test language at home. This result appears to be
1193: consistent with the other two surveys. In TIMSS at least non-native immigrants and in
1195: - 29 -
1196: PIRLS both types of immigrants face significantly educational disadvantages
1197: compared to similar native students in France, Netherlands, Sweden and Switzerland.
1198: In Germany, the immigrant-native gap in language spoken at home and SES
1199: explains largely educational achievement gaps at least for PISA and TIMSS. While
1200: holding the language variable constant, non-natives fared still worse in Germany for
1201: TIMSS and PISA the coefficient of non-natives shrinks by about one-third in PISA
1202: and appears to be insignificant in TIMSS once it is additional controlled for SES.
1203: However, in PIRLS contrary results appear indicating that primary school immigrants
1204: face still a significant educational disadvantage in Germany even if it is controlled for
1205: language and SES.
1206: Taken together, consistently across surveys immigrants do not differ greatly
1207: from natives in Australia, Canada and New Zealand in general, independent of
1208: whether we focus on unconditional results or control for language spoken at home,
1209: SES, family structure and area. A foreign language spoken at home seems to explain a
1210: large amount of immigrants' achievement differences in the UK and the USA.
1211: However, consistently across surveys28 neither SES nor foreign language can explain
1212: the total amount of immigrants' educational disadvantage in the Netherlands, France,
1213: Sweden and Switzerland. In these countries immigrants with the same socio-economic
1214: background and the same language spoken at home than natives achieve still worse
1215: than their native counterparts. For all four countries about 60 percent29 of the
1216: achievement gap between immigrants and natives remains unexplained by Model 2 in
1217: PISA, while it is about 40 percent for the countries Netherlands, Sweden and
1218: Switzerland covered by TIMSS.30
1220: 28 If we facilitated Model 2 by creating a dummy for immigrants as a whole (instead separating
1221: between non-natives and first generation immigrants) the consistency of survey results is even much
1222: clearer. Throughout all surveys immigrants are not significantly different from natives in Australia,
1223: Canada, New Zealand, UK and the USA if controlled for parental background. In France, the
1224: Netherlands, Sweden and Switzerland immigration still matters conditional on SES. Only in Germany
1225: survey results vary: while immigration status is insignificant for determining achievement in PISA and
1226: TIMSS, immigrants' achievement in PIRLS reading is still worse than that of their native counterparts
1227: with similar family background.
1228: 29 For receiving this result we decompose achievement differences between natives and immigrants into
1229: an explained and residual component (Oaxaca 1973). The percentage gives the unexplained component
1230: as a share of the total difference in achievement between immigrants and natives.
1231: 30 While in PISA and TIMSS countries do rather not differ regarding the unexplained share in the
1232: achievement gap this is different for PIRLS. With this survey the unexplained achievement gap
1233: between natives and immigrants is 35 percent for Sweden, 45 percent for France, 55 percent in
1234: Germany and 69 percent in the Netherlands.
1238: As argued in Sub-section 4.2, not only parental background impacts upon
1239: achievement but in addition special immigrant characteristics (like schooling
1240: ambitions or cultural attitudes) pupils are exposed to. This might be the reason for the
1241: considerable unexplained share of immigrants' educational achievement gap in some
1242: countries that resulted from Model 2. Hence, Model 3 questions whether a higher
1243: concentration of immigrant peers in schools is related to greater immigrants'
1244: educational disadvantage.
1245: For examining this question we calculate a variable in PISA, that gives each
1246: school's share of natives who are over-represented in the school (negative value) or
1247: share of over-represented immigrants (positive value) that would need to be shifted in
1248: order to achieve a country proportional representation of immigrants in the school.
1249: More precise, the calculation formula estimates the contribution of each school to the
1250: before described national DI31. However, it is important to note that the so calculated
1251: contribution of the school to the national DI might capture considerable `noise' given
1252: that the pupils' sample size32 per school is generally not greater than 35 and that also
1253: the school sample size is relatively small for some countries in PISA.33
1254: Adding only the schools' DI contribution variable to Model 2 could lead to an
1255: overestimation of its impact, since schools with great shares of immigrants might be
1256: attended by pupils with an on average lower socio-economic background than schools
1257: with an over-representation of natives. In order to control for this `schools' socio-
1260: (ISEI)34 of pupils' parents.

1262: `socio-economic position' is also related to a significantly (1 percent level) higher
1263: achievement of the pupil attending this school even though it is controlled for pupils'
1267: correlated, results do not change if the SRI is applied.

1277: However, what is the impact of highly segregated schools? In eight of the ten
1278: countries covered by PISA the schools dissimilarity contribution is significant at least
1279: at the 5 percent level. In seven of these countries an overrepresentation of immigrants
1280: in the school is related to lower achievement even if controlled for pupils' and
1281: schools' socio-economic background. In only one of these countries ± Australia ± the
1282: dissimilarity contribution is positive, indicating that a higher share of immigrants has
1283: a positive impact on achievement results (5 percent significance level). This result
1284: might reflect that the `immigrant capital' in Australia is favourable35.
1285: In the USA, the UK and New Zealand children in schools where immigrants
1286: are over-represented with one percent achieve about 5 to 10 points less than their
1287: counterparts in schools with a representative distribution of immigrants for the
1288: country population. Great are achievement differences of these pupils in Switzerland,
1289: Canada and Germany where differences amount to as many as 25 to 30 points.
1290: Nevertheless, even though we controlled for schools' and pupils' SES, these point
1291: differences need to be interpreted carefully. The educational system in Germany and
1292: Switzerland is shaped by a hierarchical school structure. Immigrant students are likely
1293: to attend the less prestigious school tracks, where educational achievement in general
1294: is lower than in schools with higher prestigious. Hence, in both countries the schools'
1295: dissimilarity value might be correlated greatly with the school track pupils attend and
1296: results present therefore also the generally lower achievement in schools at the bottom
1298: Nevertheless, the result on school segregation indicates that both, natives and
1299: immigrants, fare worse if immigrants are over-represented in the school. We ran the
1300: same model adding an interaction variable between the schools' dissimilarity value
1301: and migration. This variable was in all but one case36 insignificant, confirming that
1303: The results are likely to confirm the before discussed thesis (Sub-section 3.2),
1304: that immigrants' lower integration in the host countries' school environment gains
1305: additional explanatory power besides SES for explaining educational outcomes.
1307: 35 Table A5 in the appendix shows, that Australia is the only country where more immigrants' than
1308: natives' mothers have completed upper secondary education for PISA reading.
1309: 36 In the USA pupils in highly segregated schools in general but even more immigrants in highly
1310: segregated schools fare worse than pupils in other schools, if it is controlled for the schools' and pupils'
1311: SES (10 percent significant level only).
1316: However, can school segregation regarding average pupils' socio-economic
1317: background or immigrants school segregation explain the great share of immigrant
1318: educational disadvantage that remained unexplained in some countries in Model 2? In
1319: contrast to results for PISA in Model 2, once it is controlled for school segregation in
1320: three of the four countries with high immigrants' disadvantage, France, Sweden and
1321: Switzerland, first generation immigrants do not any more fare significantly worse than
1322: natives. In addition, also point differences for non-natives shrink by a third (France,
1323: Switzerland and Netherlands) or fall into insignificance (Sweden). This result
1324: indicates that school segregation does matter for explaining immigrants' lower
1325: educational achievement.
1326: Taken together, the regression analysis shed light on variation in achievement
1327: between different groups of immigrants (Model 1), the impact of SES on immigrants'
1328: achievement gap (Model 2) and the influence of school segregation (Model 3).
1329: TIMSS and PISA results for about 14 to 15 year-olds appear to be relative consistent,
1330: while PIRLS results on reading achievement of primary school children differ more
1331: greatly compared to the other two surveys.
1332: For Australia, Canada and New Zealand we find that immigrants do not differ
1333: greatly from natives regarding their educational achievement. Achievement gaps
1334: between natives and migrants in the UK and the USA diminish greatly once it is
1335: controlled for the language spoken at home. This indicates, that it is especially pupils
1336: with different language skills that drive immigrants' achievement gaps in these
1337: countries. In Germany, immigrants seem to differ from natives mainly due to their
1338: language spoken at home and their lower socio-economic status. However, migration
1339: matters still in France, the Netherlands, Sweden and Switzerland if it is controlled for
1340: language and parental background. Adding a variable to the model that captures the
1341: dissimilarity in immigrants distribution between schools decreases greatly the
1342: significance of the migration variables in these countries. Hence, in these four
1343: countries, especially socio-economic background but also the higher clustering of
1344: immigrants in schools explains the relatively large educational disadvantage of pupils
1345: whose parents were born abroad.

1397: 5 Conclusion
1398: How do immigrants differ from natives regarding educational achievement
1399: and do we receive robust results across surveys?
1400: For answering these questions we order the results of this analysis by three
1401: different reasons (discussed in detail in the introduction) why we expect immigrants'
1402: achievement to be different from that of natives.
1403: First, immigrants' socio-economic background might be different from that of
1404: natives. At the country level of analysis, we found consistently across surveys that the
1405: lower the socio-economic background of immigrants is compared to natives in the
1406: country the bigger is immigrants' educational disadvantage in this country. In
1407: Australia, Canada and New Zealand immigrants' SES is similar to that of natives.
1408: Hence, surveys show consistently no or only marginal educational disadvantage of
1409: immigrants for these three countries. On the other hand, in the Netherlands, Germany
1410: and Switzerland immigrants' composition is much less favourable than that of natives,
1411: so that in these countries immigrants fare much worse than natives regarding
1412: educational achievement.
1413: Nevertheless, once we switch from the country to the individual level by
1414: applying an OLS regression framework we find that family background is not the only
1415: factor that impacts upon immigrants' achievement. Relatively consistently across
1416: surveys in France, the Netherlands, Sweden and Switzerland immigration still matters
1417: even if pupils' socio-economic background is hold constant.
1418: What else might impact on immigrants' educational achievement then?
1419: Second, immigrants' educational disadvantage might derive from their
1420: problems of integration into the host country. One aspect of the integration issue is the
1421: pupils’ capacity to communicate in the language of the host country. Regression
1422: analysis showed consistently across surveys that speaking a foreign language at home
1423: decreases pupils' achievement greatly in all countries compared.
1424: Another aspect of integration is the time immigrants live in the host country.
1425: We expect pupils who were born and grew up in the host country (first-generation
1426: immigrants) to be better integrated into their host society than newly arrived
1427: immigrants (non-native immigrants). On the aggregated country level but also by
1428: applying the OLS regression framework, results showed that first-generation
1430: - 35 -
1431: immigrants fared much better than non-native immigrants in all countries with the
1432: exception of New Zealand. Again, this result was greatly consistent across surveys.
1433: The last aspect regarding immigrants' integration we examined was school
1434: segregation as a measure of conglomeration of immigrant peers and as an indicator
1435: for how successful countries are in integrating immigrant minorities in the school
1436: environment. In Canada, France, Germany, New Zealand, Switzerland, the UK and
1437: the USA pupils in schools with an over-representation of immigrants (compared to the
1438: national share of 15 year old immigrants) fared worse than pupils in other schools
1439: even if pupils' and schools' socio-economic background were hold constant.
1440: Obviously, high clustering of immigrants in some schools is neither favourable for the
1441: educational achievement of immigrants nor natives attending these schools. (This
1442: result however is only based on PISA data since the sample design of the other two
1443: surveys did not allow for constructing a segregation index.)
1444: Controlling for school segregation decreased immigrants' educational
1445: disadvantage in France, the Netherlands, Sweden and Switzerland where socio-
1446: economic background differences between the two groups of children could not
1447: explain the whole immigrants' educational disadvantage.
1448: Third, the process of selection of immigrants is likely to impact upon their
1449: achievement results. Immigration control but also the self-selection of immigrants
1450: determines the characteristics and motivations of immigrants. In this paper it was not
1451: possible to examine this selection issue. Our data lack even the most basic variable for
1452: doing so, the immigrants' country of origin. However, achievement data allow for
1453: examining educational dispersion of immigrants and natives separately indicating how
1454: different immigrants are compared to natives. This perspective also emphasises that
1455: ªimmigrantº is not equal to ªimmigrantº in one country. TIMSS and PISA results
1456: showed that immigrants show generally higher educational dispersion than natives.
1457: Hence, they differ even more in their educational outcomes than their native
1458: counterparts. In countries where immigrants' educational disadvantage is small
1459: immigrants at the bottom of the achievement distribution show only slightly worse
1460: performance than their lowest achieving native counterparts. However, immigrants'
1461: achievement at the bottom of their achievement distribution is much lower than that of
1462: lowest performing natives in those countries where immigrants face considerable
1463: educational disadvantages. Hence, immigrants fall even far behind the worst
1464: achieving natives in Switzerland, Germany and the Netherlands. This result is
1466: - 36 -
1467: concerning since these immigrants' chances to catch up and to integrate into the host
1468: countries' labour markets are rather small.
1470: The results of the analysis lead to some clear policy implications for fostering
1471: immigrants' educational achievement.
1472: The promotion of language skills of immigrant students speaking a foreign
1473: language is important in all countries we focused on. In countries, where socio-
1474: economic background differences are great between immigrants and natives (like
1475: Switzerland, Netherlands, Germany, France and Sweden), promoting all pupils with
1476: unfavourable family background would benefit greatly also immigrants. In addition,
1477: decreasing school segregation is likely to have a positive outcome on pupils'
1478: achievement in general and for some countries it might additionally decrease
1479: immigrants' educational achievement gap. Furthermore, in Netherlands, Germany and
1480: Switzerland a general promotion of the lowest low performing students would greatly
1481: help those immigrants who are gathered around the bottom and lost end of the
1482: achievement distribution.

9: ФАКТОРЫ, ВЛИЯЮЩИЕ НА ДОСТИЖЕНИЯ КАЗАХСТАНСКИХ
10: УЧАЩИХСЯ В МЕЖДУНАРОДНОМ ИССЛЕДОВАНИИ PISA-2018
11: Международная программа оценки школьников PISA (Programme for International Student
12: Assessment) является одним из самых авторитетных исследований в области качества школьного
13: образования. Актуальность статьи обусловлена выявлением факторов, влияющих на достижения
14: казахстанских учащихся в международном исследовании PISA-2018. В данной статье использу -
15: ется фактор-центричный исследовательский дизайн (factor-centric research design), при котором
16: основная цель – вычислить направление и размер одной или нескольких переменных влияния.
17: Основные направления и идея научного исследования связаны с результатами исследования
18: PISA, транслирующими большое количество факторов, влияющих на результаты школьников,
19: которые можно условно разделить на географические, школьные, семейно-биографические и
20: социально-экономические. Для моделирования были выбраны: индекс социально-культурно-
28: веденного исследования заключается в попытке собрать основные факторы, объясняющие ре -
29: зультаты казахстанских школьников, по таким направлениям исследования PISA-2018, как мате -
30: матика, чтение и естествознание на индивидуальном, школьном и региональном уровнях.
31: Практическое значение: анализ устойчивых факторов неравенства на протяжении 10-14 лет
36: U.A. Ospanova1,2*, K.S. Nurumov1, M.K. Atanaeva1
37: 1JSC «Information and Analytical Center», Kazakhstan, Astana
38: 2L.N. Gumilyov Eurasian National University, Kazakhstan, Astana
39: *e-mail: uljansbox@mail.ru
40: Factors influencing the achievements of Kazakhstani students
41: in the PISA-2018 international study
42: The PISA International Student Assessment Program (Program for International Student Assessment)
43: is one of the most authoritative studies in the field of school education quality.
44: The relevance of the article is due to the factors influencing the achievements of Kazakhstani stu -
45: dents in the PISA-2018 international study.
46: This article uses factor-centric research design, in which the main purpose is to calculate the direc -
47: tion and size of one or more variables of interest. The main directions and the idea of scientific research
48: are characterized by the results of the PISA study, which broadcast a large number of factors affecting
49: the results of schoolchildren, which can be divided into geographical, school, family-biographical and
50: socio-economic.
51: The scientific and practical significance of the work lies in the fact that, based on a literary review,
53: the group level.
54: The research methodology includes multilevel regression analysis, complex sampling design, statis -
55: tical analysis, multilevel linear regression, calculations of variance of random effects, the possibility of
56: adding fixed effects at the group level.
59: У .А. Оспанова и др.As the main results and analysis, as well as the conclusions of the research work, we can cite models
60: for mathematical, reading and natural science literacy of 15-year-olds, in which only a random constant
61: is calculated, without including predictors at the individual and group levels.
62: The value of the conducted research lies in the first attempt to collect the main factors explaining the
63: results of Kazakhstani schoolchildren in such areas of the PISA-2018 study as mathematics, reading and
64: natural science at the individual, school and regional levels.
65: The practical significance of the results of the work is considered in the analysis of persistent factors
66: of inequality over 10-14 years of development of the education system in the Republic of Kazakhstan.
67: Further study of the language difference and the influence of the generally accepted typology of schools
68: on the test results is recommended.
69: Keywords: school education quality, statistical analysis, plausible values, complex sampling design.

76: PISA – (Programme for International Student Assessment) халықаралық оқушыларды бағалау
77: бағдарламасы мектептегі білім беру сапасы саласындағы ең беделді зерттеулердің бірі.

79: жетістіктеріне әсер ететін факторларға байланысты.

81: мен өлшемін есептеу болып табылатын факторлық зерттеу дизайны (factor-centric research design) қолданылады.

83: көптеген факторларды көрсететін PISA зерттеу нәтижелерімен сипатталады, оларды шартты түрде географиялық, мектептік, отбасылық-өмірбаяндық және әлеуметтік-экономикалық деп бөлуге болады.

85: әдеби шолуға сүйене отырып, әлеуметтік-мәдени-экономикалық мәртебе индексі, резиленттілік көрсеткіші және т. б. таңдалды.
90: жеке және топтық деңгейлерде болжаушыларды қоспай, тек кездейсоқ тұрақты есептелетін 15 жастағы студенттердің математикалық, оқу және жаратылыстану сауаттылығына арналған модельдерді келтіруге болады.

1: NBER WORKING PAPER SERIES
2: EDUCATION FOR GROWTH: WHY AND FOR WHOM?
3: Alan B. KruegerMikael Lindahl
4: Working Paper 7591http://www.nber.org/papers/w7591NATIONAL BUREAU OF ECONOMIC RESEARCH1050 Massachusetts AvenueCambridge, MA 02138March 2000
9: Education for Growth: Why and For Whom?Alan B. Krueger and Mikael LindahiNBER Working Paper No. 7591March 2000JEL No. J24, E20
10: ABSTRACT
11: This paper tries to reconcile evidence from the mi.croeconometric and empirical macro growthliteratures on the effect of schooling on income and GDP growth. Much microeconometric evidence suggestthat education is an important causal determinant of income for individuals within countries. At a nationallevel., however, recent studies have found that increases in educational attainment are unrelated to economicgrowth. This finding appears to be a spurious result of the extremely high rate of measurement error in first-differenced cross-country education data. After accounting for measurement error, the effect of changes ineducational attainment on income growth in cross-country data is at least as great as microeconometricestimates of the rate of return to years of schooling. Another finding of the macro growth literature - thateconomic growth depends positively on the initial stock of human capital - isshown to result from imposinglinearity and constant-coefficient assumptions on the estimates. These restrictions are often rejected by thedata, and once either assumption is relaxed the initial level of education has little effect on economic growthfor the average country.
12: Alan B. Krueger Mikael LindahlEconomics Department Economics DepartmentPrinceton University Stockholm UniversityPrinceton, NJ 08540 Stockholm, Swedenand NBER
15: 1. IntroductionInterest in the rate of return to investment in education has been sparked by twoindependent developments in economic research in the 1990s. On the one hand, the micro laborliterature has produced several new estimates of the monetary return to schooling that exploitnatural experiments in which variability in workers' schooling attainment was generated by someexogenous and arguably random force, such as quirks in compulsory schooling laws or students'proximity to a college. On the other hand, the macro growth literature has investigated whetherthe level of schooling in a cross-section of countries is related to the countries' subsequent (JDPgrowth rate. This paper summarizes and tries to reconcile these two disparate but related linesof research.
16: The next section reviews the theoretical and empirical foundations of the Mincerian humancapital earnings function. Our survey of the literature indicates that Mincer's (1974) formulationof the log-linear earnings-education relationship fits the data rather well. Each additional year ofschooling appears to raise earnings by about 10 percent in the United States, although the rate ofreturn to education varies over time as well as across countries. There is surprisingly litheevidence that omitted variables (e.g., inherent ability) that might be correlated with earnings andeducation cause simple OLS estimates of wage equations to significantly overstate the return toeducation. Indeed, consistent with Griiches 's (1977) conclusion, much of the modem literaturefinds that the upward "ability bias" is of about the same order of magnitude as the downward biascaused by measurement error in educational attainment.Section 3 considers the empirical macro growth literature. First, we relate the Mincerianwage equation to the macro growth model. The Mincer model implies that the change in acountry's avenge level of schooling should be the key determinant of income growth. The macro
19: 2growth literature, by contrast, typically specifies growth as a function of the initial level ofeducation. Moreover, we show that if the return to education changes over time (e.g., becauseof exogenous skill-biased technological change), the macro growth models are unidentified. Muchof the empirical growth literature has eschewed the Mincer model because studies such asBenhabib and Spiegel (1994) fmd that the change in education is not a determinant of economicgrowth.' We show, however, that Benhabib and Spiegel's finding that increases in education areunrelated to economic growth results because there is virtually no signal in the education data theyuse, conditional on the growth of capital.In contrast to the micro education literature, the macro growth literature has devoted onlycursory attention to potential problems caused by measurement errors in education. Despite theiraggregate nature, available data on average schooling levels across countries are poorly measured,in large part because they are often derived from enrollment flows. The reliability of country-level education data is no higher than the reliability of individual-level education data. Forexample, the correlation between Barro and Lee's (1991) and Kyriacou's (1991) measures ofaverage education across 68 countries in 1985 is 0.86, and the correlation between the change inschooling between 1965 and 1985 from these two sources is only 0.34. Additional estimates ofthe reliability of country-level education data based on our analysis of comparable micro thta fromthe World Values Survey for 34 countries suggests that measurement error is particularlyprevalent for secondary and higher schooling. The measurement errors in schooling are positivelycorrelated over time, but not as highly correlated as true years of schooling. We find that
20: 'There are also notable exceptiom that have embraced the Mincer model, such as Hall and Jones (1998) and Kienow and Rodriguez-Dare (1997).
23: 3measurement errors in education severely attenuate estimates of the effect of the change inschooling on GDP growth. Nonetheless, we show that measurement errors in schooling areunlikely to cause a spurious positive association between the initial level of schooling and GDPgrowth across countries, conditional on the change in education. Thus, like Topel (1999), weconclude that both the change and initial level of education are positively correlated with economicgrowth.Finally, we explore whether the significant effect of the initial level of schooling on growthcontinues to hold if several restrictive assumptions in the growth equation are relaxed. Forexample, we estimate a variable-coefficient model that allows the coefficient on education to varyacross countries (as is found in the micro data), and we relax the linearity assumption of the initiallevel of education. These extensions show that the positive effect of the initial level of educationon economic growth is sensitive to econometric restrictions that are rejected by the data.Our main conclusion is that while support for the micro Mincerian wage equation isstrong, the evidence of a positive effect of the stock of education on a country's growth rate istenuous. Even if one accepts the strong assumptions necessary to interpret the coefficient on theinitial level of education in cross-country growth regressions as identif'ing externalities fromeducation, the results most likely do not apply to the OECD countries, or to the average countryin the world: the positive effect of the initial level of education applies primarily to countries withvery low levels of education.
24: 2. The Microeconomjcs of the Private Return to EducationSince at least the beginning of the century, economists and other social scientists have
27: 4sought to estimate the economic rewards individuals and society gain from completing higherlevels of schooling.2 It has long been recognized that workers who attended school longer maypossess other characteristics that would lead them to earn higher wages irrespective of their levelof education. If these other characteristics are not accounted for, then simple comparisons ofearnings across individuals with different levels of schooling would overstate the return toeducation. Early attempts to control for this "ability bias" included the analysis of data on siblingsto difference-out unobserved family characteristics (e.g., Gorseline, 1932), and regressionanalyses which included as control variables observed characteristics such as IQ and parentaleducation (e.g., Griiches and Mason, 1972). This literature is thorougffly surveyed in Griliches(1977), Rosen (1977), Willis (1986), and Card (1999). We briefly review evidence on theMincerian earnings equation, emphasizing recent studies that exploit exogenous variations ineducation in their estimation.
28: 2.1 The Mincerian wage equationMincer (1974) showed that if the only cost of attending school an additional year is theopportunity cost of students' time, and if the proportional increase in earnings caused by thisadditional schooling is constant over the lifetime, then the log of earnings would be linearlyrelated to individuals' years of schooling, and the slope of this relationship could be interpretedas the rate of return to investment in schooling . Heaugmented this model to include a quadratic

There are, of course, other theoretical models that yield a log-linear earnings-schooling relationship. For example, if the
32: 5termin work experience to allow for returns to on-the-job training, yielding the familiarMincerian wage equation:(1) lnW=P0+p1S+p2x1÷p3x12+€.,where In W is the natural log of the wage for individual i, Si is years of schooling, X isexper-ience, X2 is experience squared, and isa disturbance term. With Mincer's assumptions, thecoefficient on schooling, equalsthe discount rate, because schooling decisions are made byequating two present value earnings streams: one with a higher level of schooling and one witha lower level. An attractive feature of Mincer's model is that time spent in school (as opposedto degrees) is the key determinant of earnings, so data on years of schooling can be used toestimate a comparable return to education in countries with very different educational systems.Equation (1) has been estimated for most countries of the world by OLS, andthe resultsgenerally yield estimates of I31 rangingfrom .05 to .15, with slightly larger estimates for womenthan men (see Psacharopoulos, 1994). The log-linear relationship also provides a good fit to thedata, as is illustrated by the plots for the U.S., Sweden, West Germany, and East Germany inFigure i. These figures display the coefficient on dummy variables indicating each year ofschooling, controlling for experience and gender, as well as the OLS estimate of the Mincerianreturn. 36: 6in countries with dramatically different economic and educational systems .Muchresearch has addressed the question of how to interpret the education slope inequation (1). Does it reflect unobserved ability and other characteristics that are correlated witheducation, or the true reward that the labor market places on education? Is education rewardedbecause it is a signal of ability (Spence, 1973), or because it Increases productive capabilities(Becker, 1964)? Is the social return to education higher or lower than the coefficient on educationin the Mincerian wage equation? Would all individuals reap the same proportionate increase intheir earnings from attending school an extra year, or does the return to education varysystematically with individual characteristics? Definitive answers to these questions are notavailable, although the weight of the evidence clearly suggests that education is not merely aproxy for unobserved ability. For example, Griliches (1977) concludes that instead of finding theexpected positive ability bias in the return to education, 'The implied net bias is either nil ornegative" once measurement error in education is taken into account.The more recent evidence from natural experiments also supports a conclusion that omittedability does not cause upward bias the return to education (see Card, 1999 for a survey). Forexample, Angrist and Krueger (1991) observe that the combined effect of school start age cutoffsand compulsory schooling laws produces a natural experiment, in which individuals who are bornon different days of the year start school at different ages, and then reach the compulsory
37: 5Evaluating micro data for states over time in the U.S., Card and Krueger (1992) fmd that the earnings-schooling relationship is flat until the education level reached by the 2nd percentile of the education distribution, and thenbecomes log-linear. There is also some evidence of sheep-skin effects around college and high school completion (e.g., Park, 1994). Although statistical tests often reject the log-linear relationship for a large sample, the figures clearly show that the log-linear relationship provides a good approximation to the functional form. It should also be noted that Murphy and Welch (1990) find that a quartic in experience provides a better fit to the data than a quadratic.
40: 7schooling age at different grade levels. If the date of the year individuals are born is unrelatedto their inherent abilities, then, in essence, variations in schooling associated with date of birthprovide a natural experiment for estimating the benefit of obtaining extra schooling in responseto compulsory schooling laws. Using a sample of nearly one million observations from the U.S.Censuses, Angrist and Krueger fmd that men born in the beginning of the calendar year, who startschool at a relatively older age and can dropout in a lower grade, tend to obtain less schooling.This pattern onlyholds for those with a high school education or less, consistent with the viewthat compulsory schooling is responsible for the pattern. They further find that the pattern ofeducation by quarter-of-birth is mirrored by the pattern of earnings by quarter-of-birth: inparticular, individuals who are born early in the year tend to earn less, on average.6 Instrumentalvariables (IV) estimates that are identified by variability in schooling associated with quarter-of-birth suggest that the payoff to education is slightly higher than the OLS estimate.7 Angrist andKrueger conclude that the upward bias in the return to schooling is about the same order ofmagnitude as the downward bias due to measurement error in schooling.Other studies have used a variety of other sources of arguably exogenous variability inschooling to estimate the return to schooling. Harmon and Walker (1995), for example, moredirectly examine the effect of compulsory schooling by studying the effect of changes in thecompulsory schooling age in the United Kingdom, while Card (1995a) exploits variations in
41: 6Again, no such pattern holds for college graduates.7Bound, Jaeger and Baker (1995) argue that Angrist and Krueger's IV estimates are biased toward the OLS estimates because of weak instruments. 44: 8schooling attainment owing to families' proximity to a college in the U.S. Duflo (1998) usesvariation in educational attainment related to school building programs across islands in Indonesia.These three papers find that the IV estimates of the return to education that exploit a "naturalexperiment" for variability in education exceed the corresponding OLS estimates, although thedifference between the IV and OLS estimates often is not statistically significant. Ashenfelter,Harmon and Oosterbeek (1999) compile estimates from 27 studies, and find that the conventionalOLS return to schooling is .066, on average, whereas the average IV estimate is .093.We interpret this evidence as suggesting that the return to an additional year of educationobtained for reasons like compulsory schooling or school-building projects is more likely to begreater, than lower, than the conventionally-estimated return to schooling. Because the schoolinglevels of individuals who are from more disadvantaged backgrounds tend to be those who are mostaffected by the interventions examined in the literature, Lang (1993) and Card (1 995b) haveinferred that the return to an additional year of schooling is higher for individuals fromdisadvantaged families than for those from advantaged families, and suggest that such a resultfollows because disadvantaged individuals have higher discount rates.Other related evidence for the U.S. suggests the payoff to investments in education arehigher for more disadvantaged individuals. First, while studies of the effect of school resourceson student outcomes yield mixed results, there is a tendency to find more beneficial effects ofschool resources for disadvantaged students (see, for example, Summers and Wolfe, 1977,Krueger, 1999 and Rivkin, Hanushek and Kain, 1998). Second, evidence suggests that pre-school
45: 8Ashenfelter, 1-Jannon and Oosterbeek explore whether publication bias accounts for the tendancy of IV estimatesto exceed the OLS estimates. After adjusting for selection bias, however, they still fmd that the return is higher, on average, in the IV estimates than in the OLS estimates (.081 versus .064).
48: 9programs have particularly large, long-term effects for disadvantaged children in terms ofreducing crime and welfare dependence, and raising incomes (see, Barnett, 1992). Third, severalstudies have found that students from advantaged and disadvantaged backgrounds make equivalentgains on standardized tests during the school year, but children from disadvantaged backgroundsfall behind during the summer while children from advantaged backgrounds move ahead (seeEntwisle, Alexander, and Olson, 1997). And fourth, evidence suggests that college students frommore disadvantaged families benefit more from attending elite colleges than do students fromadvantaged families (see Dale and Krueger, 1998).It is unclear whether this evidence of a higher return to human capital for disadvantagedstudents applies outside the U.S. But in all regions of the world, Psacharopolous (1994)concludes that there is a higher return to primary schooling than to secondary or tertiaryschooling, which also suggests that disadvantaged children benefit more from additional humancapital investments.
49: 2.2 Social versus Private Returns to EducationThe social return to education can, of course, be higher or lower than the private return.The social return can be higher because of externalities from education, which could occur, forexample, if higher education leads to technological progress that is not captured in the privatereturn to that education, or if more education produces positive externalities, such as a reductionin crime and welfare participation, or more informed political decisions. The former is morelikely if human capital is expanded at higher levels of education while the latter is more likely ifit is expanded at lower levels. It is also possible that the social return to education is less than the
52: 10private return. For example, Spence (1973) and Machlup (1970) note that education could justbe a credential, which does not raise individuals' productivities. It is also possible that in somedeveloping countries, where the incidence of unemployment may rise with education (e.g., Blaug,Layard and Woodhall, 1969) and where the return to physical capital may exceed the return tohuman capital (e.g., Harberger, 1965), increases in education may reduce total output.Most of the micro human capital literature focuses on the private rather than social returnto education, but the fmding of little ability bias in the Mincerian wage equation casts doubt onat least some forms of credentialling arguments. The possibility of externalities to educationmotivates much of the macro growth literature, to which we now turn.
53: 3. Macro growth equationsThirty years ago, Fritz Machlup (1970, p. 1) observed, "The literature on the subject ofeducation and economic growth is some two hundred years old, but only in the last ten years hasthe flow of publications taken on the aspects of a flood." The number of cross-country regressionstudies on education and growth has surged even higher in recent years. The literature isvoluminous, and a new journal has been devoted solely to economic growth. Rather thanexhaustively review the literature, we summarize the main models and findings, and explore theimpact of several econometric issues.
54: The macro growth literature yields three principally different conclusions from the microliterature. First, the initial stock of human capital matters, not the change in human capital.Second, secondary and post-secondary education matter more for growth than primary education.Third, female education has an insignificant and sometimes negative effect on economic growth.

Ifthe return to schooling has increased(decreased) secularly over time, the initial level of education will enter positively (negatively) intoequation (5).Animplicit assumption in much of the macro growth literature therefore is that thereturn to education is either unchanged, or changed endogenously, by the stock of human capital.Although the empirical literature for the U.S. clearly shows a fall in the return to educationin the 1970s and a sharp increase in the 1980s (e.g., Levy and Murnane, 1992), the findings forother countries are mixed. 70: 3.1.1 InterpretationThere are at least six ways to interpret the coefficient on the initial level of schooling inequation (6).12First,schooling may be a proxy for steady-state income. Countries with moreschooling would be expected to have a higher steady-state income, so conditional on GDP in theinitial year, we would expect more educated countries to grow faster (j32 >0). 13Ifthis were thecase, higher schooling levels would not change the steady-state growth rate, although it wouldraise steady-state income. Second, schooling could change the steady-state growth rate byenabling the work force to develop, implement and adopt new technologies (see Nelson andPhelps, 1966 Welch, 1970 and Romer, 1990), again leading to the prediction >0.Third,countries with low initial stocks of human capital could have greater opportunities to grow by
71: ttHeckman andKienow(1997) point out that halfthevariance of log incomewill be added to the GDP equation if income is log normal.
72: 22SeeAghionandHowitt(1998) and Temple (1999a) for excellent overviews of growth models.'3Transitory measurement error in GDP will have the same effect.
75: 15implementing technology developed abroad. In this case, one would expect <o AghionandHowjtt (1998) and Benhabib and Spiegel (1994) suggest, however, that for countries with below-average productivity and income, this "catch-up" effect would be more prominent among thosewith higher average educational attainment, yielding the prediction f32 >0for this subset ofcountries.Fourth, a positive or negative coefficient on initial schooling may simply reflect anexogenous change in the return to schooling, as shown in equation (5). Fifth, anticipatedincreases in future economic growth could cause schooling to rise (i.e., reverse causality), asargued by Bils and Klenow (1998). Sixth, the schooling variable may "pick up' the effect of thechange in education, which is typically omitted from the growth equation.

Indeed, we show belowthat conditional on the other variables that Benhabib and Spiegel hold constant (especially capital),the change in schooling conveys virtually no signal.'7Notice also that the coefficient on capital is high in Table 1, around .50 witha t-ratio closeto 10.Ina competitive, Cobb-Douglas economy, the coefficient on capital growth in a GDPgrowth regression should equal capital's share of national income. Gollin (1998) estimates thatlabor's share ranges from .65to.80 in most countries, after allocating labor's portion of self-employment and proprietors'income. Consequently, capital's share is probably no higher than.20 to .35. Two reasons why the coefficient on capital could be biased upwards are: (1)measured capital is derived from investment flows, and GDP is a direct function of investment,so errors in the investment data will be positively correlated with the dependent variable; (2)countries that experience rapid GDP growth may find it easier to raise investment, creatingsimultaneity bias. These factors might explain why capital has such a large and significantcoefficient in the growth equations. If the coefficient on capital growth in column (5)ofTable1 is constrained to equal .20 or .35 -- aplausible range for capital's share -- thecoefficient on theschooling change rises to .09 or .06, and becomes statistically significant.

88: 183.2.1 The Extent of Measurement Error in International Education DataWe disregard errors that arise because years of schooling are an imperfect measure ofhuman capital, and focus instead on the more tractable problem of estimating the extent ofmeasurement error in cross-country data on average years of schooling. Benhabib and Spiegel'smeasure of average years of schooling for the work force was derived by Kyriacou (1991) asfollows. First, survey-based estimates of average years of schooling for 42 countries in the midI 970s were regressed on the countries primary, secondary and tertiary school enrollment rates.Coefficient estimates from this model were then used to predict years of schooling fromenrollment rates for all countries in 1965 and 1985. This method is likely to generate substantialnoise since the fitted regression may not hold for all countries and time periods, enrollment ratesare frequently rnismeasured, and the enrollment rates are not properly aligned with the workforce.Changes in education derived from this measure are likely to be particularly noisy. Benhabib andSpiegel use Kyriacou's education data for 1965, as well as the change between 1965 and 1985.The widely-used Barro and Lee (1993) data set is an alternative source of education data.For 40 percent of country-year cells, Barro and Lee measure average years of schooling bysurvey- and census-based estimates reported by UNESCO. The remaining observations werederived from historical enrollment flow data using a "perpetual inventory method.''8 The Barro-Lee measure is undoubtedly an advance over existing international measures of educationalattainment, but errors in measurement are inevitable because the UNESCO enrollment rates areof doubtful quality in many countries (see Behrman and Rosensweig, 1993 and 1994). For
89: '8Each countty has a survey- and census-based estimate in at least one year, which provides an anchor for the enrollment flows.
92: 19example, UNESCO data are often based on beginning of the year enrollment. Additionally,students educated abroad are miscounted in the flow data, which is probably a larger problem forhigher education. More fundamentally, secondary and tertiary schooling is defined differentlyacross countries in the UNESCO data, so years of secondary and higher schooling are likely tobe noisier than overall schooling. Notice also that because errors cumulate over time in Barro andLee's stock-flow calculations, the errors in education will be positively correlated over time.As is well known, if an explanatory variable is measured with additive white noise errors,then the coefficient on this variable will be attenuated toward zero in a bivariate regression, withthe attenuation factor, R, asymptotically equal to the ratio of the variance of the correctly-measured variable to the variance of the observed variable (see, e.g., Griliches, 1986). A similarresult holds in a multiple regression (with correctly-measured covariates), only now the variancesare conditional on the other variables in the model. To estimate attenuation bias due tomeasurement error, write a nation's measured years of schooling, S, as it's true schooling, SfK,plus a measurement error denoted e: S =S+ ej. It is convenient to start with the assumptionthat the measurement errors are "classical"; that is, errors that are uncorrelated with 5*, othervariables in the growth equation, and the equation error term. Now let S' and 52 denotetwoimperfect measures of average years of schooling for each country, with measurement errors &ande2 respectively (where we suppress the j subscript).If e' and e2 are uncorrelated, the fraction of the observed variability in 51 due tomeasurement error can be estimated as R1 = cov(S',S2)/var(S').R1 is often referred to as thereliability ratio of 1,andhas probability limit equal to var(S*)/lvar(S*)+var(e5}. Assumingconstant variances, the reliability of the data expressed in changes (R) will be lower than the
95: 20cross-sectional reliability if the serial correlation of the true variable is higher than the serialcorrelation of the measurement errors because R51 = var(S*)/{var(S*)+ var(e)(1-rj/(1-p,)},where r is the serial correlation of the errors and p is the serial correlation of true schooling.In practice, the reliability ratio for changes in S1 can be estimated by: RAS1 =cov(AS'4S2)/var(aS').Note that if the errors in 5' and S 2are positively correlated, the estimatedreliability ratios will be biased upward.We can calculate the reliability of the Barro-Lee and Kyriacou data if we treat the twovariables as independent estimates of educational attainment. It is probably the case, however,that the measurement errors in the two data sources are positively correlated because, to someextent, they both rely on the same mismeasured enrollment data)9 Consequently, the reliabilityratios derived from comparing these two measures probably provide an upper bound on thereliability of the data series.

To derive ameasure of education with independent errors, we calculated average years of schooling from theWorld Values Survey (WVS) for 34 countries. The WVS containsmicro data from householdsurveys that were conducted in nearly 40 countries in 1990 or 1991. The survey was designedto be comparable across countries. In each country, individuals were asked to report the age atwhich they left school. With an assumption of school start age, we can calculate the averagenumber of years that individuals spent in school. We also calculated average years of secondaryand higher schooling by counting years of schooling obtained after 8 years of schooling assecondary and higher schooling. Notice that these measures will not be error free either. Errorscould arise, for example, because some individuals repeated gndes, because we have made anerroneous assumption about school start age or the beginning of secondary schooling, or becauseof sampling errors. But the errors in this measure should be independent of the errors inKyriacou' s and Barro and Lee's data. The appendix provides additional details of our calculationswith the WVS.

111: 3.3 Additional Growth Models
112: Measurement errors aside, one could question whether physical capital should be includedin a GDP growth equation because it is potentially an endogenous variable. Fast growingcountries have more access to capital (Blomstrom, Lipsey, and Zejan, 1993). Additionally,considerations of the low signal in schooling changes conditional on capital growth, and themechanical correlation between measured capital and GDP (since capital is typically derived frominvestment), lead us to prefer parsimonious models that omit capital. Barro (1997) also excludescapital, so there is precedent for a parsimonious specification in the growth literature. We firstreport models without controlling for capital, and then focus on the effect of capital in long-difference models in Section 3.6.Table 3 reports "stylized" macro growth models without controlling for physical capitalfor samples spanning 5, 10 or 20 year periods. The dependent variable is the annualized changein the log of real GDP per capita per year based on Sununers and Heston's (1991) Penn WorldTables, Mark 5:6. Results are generally similar if GDP per worker is used instead. We use GDPper capita because it reflects labor force participation decisions and because it has been the focusof much of the previous literature. The schooling variable is Barro and Lee's measure of average

However, increases in average years of schooling have a positive and statisticallysignificant effect on economic growth over periods of 10 or 20 years. The magnitude of thecoefficient estimates on both the change and initial level of schooling over long periods are large,probably too large to represent the causal effect of schooling.The fmding that the time span matters so much for the change in education suggests thatmeasurement error in schooling influences these estimates. Over short time periods, there is littlechange in a nation's true mean schooling level, so the transitory component of measurement errorin schooling would be large relative to variability in the true change. 121: 26changes. Measurement error bias appears to be greater over the 5 and 10 year horizons, but itis still substantial over 20 years.
