122: Like Benhabib and Spiegel, Barro and Sala-i-Martin (1995) conclude that contemporaneouschanges in schooling do not contribute to economic growth. There are four reasons to doubt theirconclusion, however. First, Barro and Sala-i-Martin analyze a mixed sample that combineschanges over both 5-year (1985-90) and 10-year (1965-75 and 1975-85) periods; examiningchanges over such short periods tends to exacerbate the downward bias due to measurementerrors. Second, they examine changes in average years of secondary and higher schooling. Aswas shown in Table 2, the cross-sectional reliability of secondary and higher schooling is lowerthan the reliability of all years of schooling, and the changes are likely to be less reliable as well.Third, they include separate variables for changes in male and female years of secondary andhigher schooling. These two variables are highly correlated (r= .85), which would exacerbate
125: 27measurement error problems if the signal in the variables is more highly correlated than the noise.If average years of secondary and higher schooling for men and women combined, or years ofsecondary and higher schooling for either men or women, is used instead of all years of schoolingin the 10-year change model in column 6 of Table 3, the change in education has a sizable,statistically significant effect. Fourth, they estimate a restricted Seemingly Unrelated Regression(SUR) system, which exacerbates measurement error bias because asymptotically this estimatoris equivalent to a weighted average of an OLS and fixed-effects estimator.Barro (1997) stresses the importance of male, secondary and higher education as adeterminant of GDP growth. In his analysis, female secondary and higher education is negativelyrelated to growth. We have explored the sensitivity of the estimates to using different measuresof education: namely, primary versus higher education, and male versus female education. Whenwe test for different effects of years of primary and secondary and higher schooling in the modelin colunm 6 of Table 3, we cannot reject that all years of schooling have the same effect on GDPgrowth (p-value equals .40 for initial levels and .12 for changes). We also find insignificantdifferences between primary and secondary schooling if we just use male schooling. We do findsignificant differences if we further disaggregate schooling levels by gender, however. The initiallevel of primary schooling has a positive effect for women and a negative effect for men, theinitial level of secondary school has a negative effect for women and a positive effect for men, thechange in primary schooling has a positive effect for women and a negative effect for men, andthe change in secondary schooling has a negative effect for women and a positive effect for men.Caselli, Esquivel and Lefort (1996) also examine the differential effect of male and femaleeducation on growth over five year intervals. They estimate a fixed effects variant of equation
128: 28(6), and instrument for initial education and GDP with their lags. Contrary to Barro, they findthat female education has a positive and statistically significant effect on growth, while maleeducation has a negative and statistically significant effect. This result appears to stem from theintroduction of fixed effects: if we estimate the model with fixed effects bitt without instrumentingfor education, we find the same gender pattern, whereas if we estimate the model without fixedeffects and instrument with lags the results are similar to Barro 's. Although country fixed effectsarguably belong in the growth equation, it is particularly difficult to untangle any differentialeffects of male and female education in such a specification because measurement error isexacerbated.24 But Caselli, Esquivel and Lefort's findings are consistent with the micro-econometric literature, which fmds education has a higher return for women than men.We conclude that because schooling levels are highly correlated for men and women, oneneeds to be cautious interpreting the effect of education in models that disaggregate education bygender and level of schooling. For this reason, and because the total number of years of educationis the variable specified in the Mincer model, we have a preference for using the average of allyears of schooling for men and women combined in our econometric analysis.
129: 3.4 Initial Level of EducationThe effect of the initial level of education on growth has been widely interpreted as anindication of large externalities from the stock of a nation's human capital on growth. Benhabiband Spiegel (1994; p. 160), for example, conclude, "The results suggest that the role of human
130: 24Note that instrumenting with lagged education does not solve the measurement error problem because we fmd that measurement errors in education are highly correlated over time.
133: 29capital is indeed one of facilitating adoption of technology from abroad and creation of appropriatedomestic technologies rather than entering on its own as a factor of production." And Barro(1997, p. 19) observes, "On impact, an extra year of male upper-level schooling is thereforeestimated to raise the growth rate by a substantial 1.2 percentage points per year." Topel (1999),however, argues that "the magnitude of the effect of education on growth is vastly too large to beinterpreted as a causal force." Indeed, Topel calculates that the present value of a one percentagepoint faster growth rate from an additional year of schooling would be about four times the cost,with a 5percentreal discount rate. He concludes that externalities from schooling may exist, butthey are unlikely to be so large. One possibility -- whichwe explore and end up rejecting -- isthat level of schooling is spuriously reflecting the effect of the change in schooling on growth.Countries with higher initial levels of schooling tended to have larger increases inschooling over the next 10 or 20 years in Barro and Lee's data, which is remarkable given thatmeasurement error in schooling will induce a negative covariance between the change and initiallevel of schooling. We initially suspected that the base level of schooling spuriously picks up theeffect of schooling increases, either because schooling changes are excluded from the growthequation or because the included variable is noisy. 151: 3.5 Measurement Error in GDPAnother possibility is that transitory measurement errors in GDP explain why initialschooling matters in the growth equation. Intuitively, this would work as follows: If a countryhas a low level of education for its measured GDP, it is likely that its true GDP is less than itsmeasured GDP. If the error in GDP is transitory, then subsequent GDP growth will appearparticularly strong for such a country because the negative error in the GDP is unlikely to repeatin the second period. One indication that this may contribute to the strong effect of the level ofeducation comes from including second period GDP instead of initial GDP in the growth equation.In this situation, measurement errors in GDP would be expected to have the opposite effect on theinitial level of education. Md indeed, if second period GDP is included instead of initial GDP

First, in Table 3 and Table 4 it isclear that the initial level of education has a significant effect even when initial GDP is not heldconstant. Second, using the WVS, we calculated the reliability of the Summers and Heston GDPdata for 1990. Specifically, to estimate the reliability of log GDP, R, we regressed the log ofreal income per person in the WVS on the log of real GDP per capita in the Summers and Hestondata. The resulting coefficient was .92 (t-ratio= 12.3), indicating substantial signal. Bothmeasures were deflated by the same PPP measure in these calculations, which may inflate thereliability estimate, but if we add log PPP as an additional explanatory variable to the regressionthe reliability of the GDP data is .89 (t-ratio= 11.9). Although the WVS income data neglect non-household income and these estimates are based on just 17 countries, the results indicate thatSummers and Heston s data convey a fair amount of signal, and that the errors in GDP are highlyserially correlated. If we assume that R is .92 and the serial correlation in the errors is .5, thecoefficient on initial education in the 10-year GDP growth equation would be biased upward byabout a third.3°

160: 343.6 The Effect of Physical CapitalThe level and growth rate of capital are natural control variables to include in the GDPgrowth regressions. First, initial log GDP can be substituted for capital in a Solow growth modelonly if capital s share is constant over time and across countries (e.g., a Cobb-Douglas productionfunction). Second, and more importantly for our purposes, capital-skill complementarity wouldimply that some of the increased output attributed to higher education in Table 3 should beattributed to increased capital (see, e.g., Goldin and Katz, 1997 on capital-skill complementarity).As mentioned earlier, however, systematic correlation between measurement errors in capital andGDP, as well as endogeneity of capital, are reasons to be wary about including the growth ofcapital in a GDP equation. Here we examine the robustness of our results to controlling forphysical capital.Column (1) of Tables reports an estimate of the same 20-year growth model as in column9 of Table 3, augmented to include the growth of capital per worker. 170: We draw four main lessons from this investigation of the role of capital. First, the changein capital has an enormous effect in a GDP growth equation, probably because of a mechanicalrelationship between the errors in measuring capital and GDP or reverse causality. Second, theimpact of both the level and change in schooling on economic growth is sensitive to whether thechange in capital is included in the growth equation nc1 allowedto have a coefficient that greatlyexceeds capital's share. Third, controlling for capital exacerbates measurement error problemsin schooling. Instrumental variables estimates designed to correct for measurement error inschooling yield such a large standard error on the change in schooling that the results areconsistent with schooling changes having no effect on growth or a large effect on growth. Fourth,when the coefficient on capital growth is constrained to equal a plausible value, changes in yearsof schooling are positively related to economic growth. Overall, unless measurement errorproblems in schooling and capital are overcome, we doubt the cross-country growth equations that
171: 34Aside from the different data sources, the difference between our IV results and Pritchett's appears to result from his use of log schooling changes. If we use log schooling changes, we also find negative point estimates.
174: 37control for capital growth will be very informative insofar as the benefit of education isconcerned.
175: 4. Less Restrictive Macro Growth ModelThe macro growth equations impose the restriction that all countries have the samerelationship between growth and initial education, and that the relationship is linear. The firstassumption is particularly worrisome because the micro evidence indicates that the return toschooling varies considerably across countries, and even across regions within countries. Forexample, institutional factors that compress the wage structure in some countries result in lowerreturns to schooling in those countries (see, e.g., the essays in Freeman and Katz, 1995).Moreover, one might expect externalities from education to be greater in countries where theprivate return is depressed below the world market level. Thus, we first allow the effect of thestock of education on growth to vary by country. Next, we examine the effect of the linearityassumption. Both of these extensions to the standard growth specification suggest that theconstrained specification estimated in the literature should be viewed with caution; qualitativelydifferent results are obtained when the constraints are relaxed.
176: 4.1 Heterogeneous Country Effects of EducationConsider the following variable-coefficient version of the macro growth equation:(11) AY =P0 +P1Sj.1+where we allow each country to have a separate schooling coefficient (P1)andignore othercovariates. If there is more than one observation per country, equation (11) can be estimated by
179: 38interacting education with a set of dummy variables indicating each country. It is instructive tonote that the coefficient on education (b1) estimated from the restricted OLS modelwith ahomogenous education slope can be written as a weighted avenge of the country-specific slopes:
180: (12)= z= •,, (S-S)2_—E1,,_1—S)2
181: wherethe weights are the country-specific contributions to the variance in schooling. Of course,if the assumptions of the constant-coefficient model hold (and the other Gauss-Markovassumptions hold), the wj weights are the most efficient weights. But if a variable-coefficientmodel is more appropriate, there is no a priori reason to prefer the OLS weights over otherweights. Indeed, it is rather odd to weight the country-specific slopes by the OLS weights if theslopes differ across countries. Probably a more relevant single estimate would be the unweighted-average coefficient, which represents the expected value of the education coefficient for countriesin the world. Lastly, notice that if equation (11) is augmented to include covariates the simpleweighted avenge interpretation of the constant-coefficient model in (12) does not apply, but theaverage of the country-specific coefficients is still informative.35Table 6 summarizes estimates of a variable-coefficient model using 5-year and 10-yearchanges in GDP. 185: 39constant-coefficient model, whereas columns 2 and 4 report the mean of the country-specificeducation coefficients. The constant education slope assumption is overwhelmingly rejected bythe data for each time period (p-value C 0.0001).Indeed, the R2 of the equations more thandoubles when the education slopes are unconstrained. Of more consequence, the average slopecoefficient is negative, though not statistically significant, in the variable-coefficient model. Theseresults cast doubt on the interpretation of initial education in the constrained macro growthequation common in the literature.Panel B of Table 6 reports results in which average years of secondary and higherschooling for males is used instead of average years of all education for the entire adultpopulation. This variable has been emphasized as a key determinant of economic growth inBarro' s work. Again, however, the results of the constant-coefficient model are qualitativelydifferent than those of the variable-coefficient model. Indeed, for the average country in thesample, a greater initial level of secondary and higher education has a statistically significant,negative association with economic growth over the ensuing 10 years.The estimates reported in Table 6 exclude the change in education to focus on the effectof the initial stock of education. We have, however, experimented with a variable-coefficientmodel for the 10-year change in education variable. These estimates were quite fragile. Forexample, if we regress annual GDP growth over ten years on a set of interactions between the 10-year change in education and country dummies, the initial log of GDP per capita, and timedummies, the average coefficient for the change in education interactions is negative. But if wealso include a set of interactions between initial education and country dummies in this model, theaverage coefficient on the change in education swells to .18, and the avenge coefficient on initial
188: 40education is negative. Since the latter model uses three observations to estimate two parametersfor each country, we are reluctant to stress these results.It is clear from Table 6 that education has a heterogenous effect on economic growthacross countries. What bearing does this result have on the convergence• literature? Lee, Pesaranand Smith (1998) show that country heterogeneity in technological progress that is assumedhomogeneous across countries in a fixed-effects model with a lagged dependent variable willgenerate a spurious correlation between the lagged dependent variable and the error term. Asimilar result will follow if heterogeneous education coefficients are constrained to equal aconstant coefficient, so we would regard the convergence coefficient with some caution since itdepends on controlling for S. Nonetheless, it is worth emphasizing that we still obtain anegative average coefficient on education if we drop initial GDP from the variable-coefficientmodel. Because we are interested in understanding the role of education in economic growth, wedo not pursue the convergence issue further, but we think the results of the variable-coefficientmodel reinforce Lee, Pesaran, and Smith's skeptical interpretation of the conventional estimateof the convergence parameter.
189: 4.2 The Importance of LinearityIt is common in the empirical growth literature to assume that initial education has a lineareffect on subsequent GDP growth. Although Mincer (1974) provides conditions under whicheducation has a linear relationship with log earnings, these conditions do not necessarily implythat the level of initial education has a linear relationship with income growth. The impact of thelinearity assumption has not been rigorously tested in growth models. As a first approach to test
192: 41this assumption, we created a set of dummy variables which indicated whether each country'sinitial average years of education fell in the 0-1 range, 1-2 range, 2-3 range, and so on. We thenincluded these dummies in lieu of linear education in the growth regression in colunm (4) of Table3. Figure 2 displays the dummy coefficients (i.e., the unrestricted relationship) and the estimatedlinear relationship between GDP growth and education. The education-CiDP growth relationshipappears upward sloping at low levels of education, then plateaus between 4 and 8 years ofeducation, and becomes downward sloping above 8 years of education.The increment to GDP growth at each level of average education is shown on the bottomof Figure 2 (i.e., change in the unrestricted relationship). Loosely speaking, the OLS regressioncoefficient estimate for education in the linear specification can be thought of as a weightedaverage of the slope increments at each level of education.36 The OLS estimate places moreweight on the slope increment at a particular schooling level if: (1) there is a greater gap inaverage years of schooling between countries above and below that schooling level; (2) theschooling level is close to the median schooling level. Figure 3A displays the weights applied tothe increments by OLS for the linear specification in a model without other covariates. Forcomparison, Figures 3B and 3C show histograms of years of schooling for all countries and forOECD countries.
193: As in the previous subsection, the OLS weighting scheme is optimal if there is a constantslope at each schooling level. But if the relationship is not linear over the range of schooling, theOLS weights may distort the relationship, and other weighting schemes may be more appropriate.
194: 36This discussion is only approximate for two reasons. First, average years of education is not a categorical variable; we have transformed it to one. Second, if there are covariates, the relationship is more complicated. For more details, see Yitzhaki (1996) and Angrist and Krueger (1999).

202: 435. ConclusionThe micro and macro literatures both emphasize the role of education in income growth.An accumulation of research using individual-level education and income data since the beginningof the 20th century provides robust evidence of a substantial payoff to investment in education,especially for those who traditionally complete low levels of schooling. From the micro evidence,it is unclear whether the social return to schooling exceeds the private return, although availableevidence suggests that positive externalities in the form of reduced crime and reduced welfareparticipation are more likely to be reaped from investments in disadvantaged than advantagedgroups (e.g., Heckman and Klenow, 1997). The macroeconomic evidence of externalities interms of technological progress from investments in higher education seems to us more fragile,resulting from imposing constant-coefficient and linearity restrictions that are rejected by the data.Our findings also help resolve an important inconsistency between the micro and macroliteratures on education: Contrary to Benhabib and Spiegel's (1994) and Barro and Sala-i-Martin's(1995) conclusions, the cross-country regressions indicate that the change in education ispositively associated with economic growth once measurement error in education is accounted for.Indeed, after adjusting for measurement error, the change in average years of schooling often hasa greater effect in the cross-country regressions than in the within-country micro regressions. Thelarger return to schooling found in the cross-country models suggests that reverse causality oromitted variables create problems at the country level of analysis, or that increases in averageeducational attainment generate nationwide externalities. Although the microeconometric evidencein several countries suggests that within countries the causal effect of education on earnings canbe estimated reasonably well by taking education as exogenous, it does not follow that cross-
205: 44country differences in education can be taken as a cause of income as opposed to a result ofcurrent income or anticipated income growth. Moreover, countries that improve their educationalsystems are likely to concurrentiy change other policies that enhance growth, possibly producinga different source of omitted-variable bias in cross-country analyses.Education, as Harbison and Myers (1965) stress, "is both the seed and the flower ofeconomic development." It is difficult to separate the causal effect of education from the positiveincome demand for education in cross-country data. Mankiw (1997) describes the presumedexogeneity of school enrollment as the 'weak link" in the empirical growth literature. In ouropinion, this link is unlikely to be strengthened unless researchers can identify natural experimentsin schooling attainment similar to those that have been exploited in the microeconometricliterature, and unless measurement errors in the cross-country data are explicitly taken intoaccount in the econometric modelling. In view of the difficulties in obtaining accurate country-level data on changes in educational attainment, it might be preferrable to examine growth acrossregions of countries with reliable data. Acemoglu and Angrist (1999), who look across U.S.states, and Moretti (1998), who looks across U.S. citites, provide good starts down this path,although they reach conflicting conclusions regarding any deviation between the social and privatereturns to education.

231: Data AppendixThe second wave of the World Values Survey (WVS) was conducted in 42 countries between1990 and 1993. The sampled countries represented almost 70% of the world population, including several countries where micro data normally are unavailable. The survey was designed by the World Values Study Group (1994), and conducted by local survey organizations (mainly Gallup) in each of the surveyed countries. Inmost countries, a national random sample of adults(over age 18) was surveyed. For 12 of the countries in our sample (Belgium, Brazil, Canada,China, India, Italy, Netherlands, Portugal, Spain, Switzerland, West Germany and U.K.),sampling weights were available to make the survey representative of the country's population;the other samples are self-weighting. A feature of the survey is that the questiolmaire wasdesigned to be similar in all countries to facilitate comparisons across countries. There are, however, drawbacks to using the V/VS for our purposes. The primary purpose of the WVS wasto compare values and norms across different societies. Although questions about income andeducation were included, they appear to have been a lower priority than the normative questions. For example, family income was collected as a categorical variable in ten ranges, and somecountries failed to report the currency values associated with the ranges. We were able to derivecomparable data from the WVS on mean years of schooling for 34 countries and on meanincome for 17 countries.
232: Mean years of schooling is calculated from question V356 in the WVS, which asked, "At whatage did you or will you complete your formal education, either at school or at an institution ofhigher education? Please exclude apprenticeships." The variable is typically bottom coded at 12years of age and top coded at 21 years of age. Although there are some benefits of formulatingthe question this way, for our purposes it also creates some problems. First, we do not know theage at which respondents started their education. For this reason we have used data fromUNESCO (1967) on the typical school starting age in each country. Second, the top and bottomcoding is potentially a problem. For almost one third of the countries (Austria, Brazil, Denmark,India, Norway, Poland, South Korea, Sweden, Switzerland and Turkey), however, a question wasasked concerning formal educational attainment. Since, as mentioned above, one of the benefitswith the V/VS is that the same questions are asked in all the countries, we used this variableonly to solve the bottom and top coding problem.' We have coded illiterate/no schooling as 0years of schooling and incomplete primary schooling as 3 years. In the two countries wheregraduate studies is a separate category, we have set this to 19. For the countries in which theeducational attainment variable does not exist, we set years of schooling for those in the bottom-
233: 'For South Korea and Switzerland, however, we exclusively used this variable to derive years of schooling because the question about school leaving age is not asked in these countries For Turkey, school leaving age is only coded as three possible ages, so we use both the educational attainment and school leaving age variable to derive years of schooling.
236: coded category equal to the midpoint of 0 and the bottom coded years of schooling.2 Similarly, we set years of schooling for the highest category equal to the midpoint of 18 and the top coded years of schooling.As mentioned, the family income variable in WVS is reported in 10 categories. We coded incomeas the midpoints of the range in each category. This variable is also censored from below and above.For simplicity, we set income for those who were bottom coded at the midpoint between zero and the lower income limit. We handled top coding by fitting a Pareto distribution to family income above each country's median income. Assuming that this distribution correctly characterizes the highest category, we calculated the mean of the censored distribution. We converted the family income variable to a dollar-value equivalent by multiplying the family income variable in eachcountry by the ratio of the purchasing power parity in dollars to the corresponding local currency exchange, using Summers and Heston's (1991) data.The logarithm of mean income per capita was calculated as the logarithm of the sum of familyincome in common currency divided by the total number of individuals in all households in thesample. The total number of individuals in each household is calculated as the sum of the numberof chilthen living at home and the number of adults present. (Two adults were assumed to be presentif the respondent was married; otherwise, one adult was assumed to be present.) Appendix Table A2 reports the weighted mean years of schooling and log income per capita derived from the WVS. The weights for these calculations were the sampling weights reported in the WVS. The sample size used to calculate mean schooling is also reported.
237: 2East and West Germany the bottom code was 14, and for Finland itwas 15. Because schoolstartage was 7 in Finland and East Germany, and 6 in West Germany, we set years of schooling equal to 6 in West Gennany and Finland and 5 in East Germany for those who were bottom coded.

146: Of the OECD countries that participate in the Programme for International Student Assessment
147: (PISA), Turkey has one of the lowest levels of performance and the highest rates of improvement in PISA
148: scores between 2003 and 2012 . New evidence presented in this paper suggests that existing accounts have
149: underestimated both progress and inequity in Turkey because they did not take into account the large
150: proportion of 15 -16 year -olds that are ineligible for the PISA sampling frame, either because they are no
151: longer in school or because they are severely delay ed. Using Turkey’s Demographic and Health Survey
152: (DHS) data for 2003, 2008 and 2013 we show that the proportion of 15 -16 year -olds that were eligible for
153: the PISA sample in Turkey has nearly doubled from 45% in PISA 2003, to 80% in PISA 2012. By
154: combining DHS data on access/attainment and PISA survey data on learning outcomes we show that: (1)
155: the improvement in the percentage of 15 -16 year -olds reaching Level 2 in PISA (functional literacy and
156: functional numeracy) is up to twice as large as that reflected in official PISA reports, (2) the gap in
157: functional literacy rates between rich and poor youth in 2012 is 2.3 times as large as was previously
158: thought, and (3 ) contrary to earlier research the gap between rich and poor has not declined between 2003
159: and 2012. The paper emphasises the importance of accounting for sample eligibility and representivi ty
160: when making inter -country and inter -temporal comparisons using international assessment data,
161: particularly for developing countries with expanding education systems.
163: Des pays de l’OCDE ayant participé au Programme international pour le suivi des acquis des élèves
164: (PISA), la Turquie obtient un des niveau de performance les plus bas et un des taux d’amélioration le plus
165: élevé dans les scores PISA entre 2003 et 2012.De nouvelles preuves dans cet article suggères que le s récits
166: existant sous -estiment à la fois le progrès et l’inégalité en Turquie car ils ne prennent pas en compte la
167: proportion importante de jeunes de 15 -16 ans qui ne sont pas éligibles dans l’échantillonnage PISA, soit
168: parce qu’ils ne sont plus scolarisé s soit parce qu’ils ont un retard important. En se servant de l’enquête
169: démographique et de santé pour la Turquie en 2003, 2008 et 2013, nous démontrons que la proportion des
170: 15-16 ans qui étaient en réalité éligible à l’échantillon PISA en Turquie a presq ue doublé allant de 45% au
171: moment de l’enquête PISA 2003, à 80% dans PISA 2012. En combinant les données de l’enquête
172: démographique sur l’accès/obtention et les données PISA sur les acquis d’apprentissage nous démontrons
173: que : (1) l’amélioration dans le po urcentage des 15 -16 ans ayant atteint le Niveau 2 dans PISA
174: (l’alphabétisation fonctionnelle et la numératie fonctionnelle) est deux fois plus important que ce qui est
176: EDU/WKP(2017)3
177: 4 reflété dans les rapports de PISA, (2) l’écart dans les taux d’alphabétisation fonctionn elle entre les riches et
178: les pauvres en 2012 est 2.3 fois plus important qu’il n’était pensé et (3) contrairement aux recherches
179: antérieurs sur l’écart entre les riches et les pauvres n’a pas baissé entre 2003 et 2012. Cet article accentue
180: l’importance de prendre en compte l’éligibilité dans l’échantillonnage et la représentation lorsqu’on
181: effectue des comparaisons inter -pays et inter -temporaires en utilisant la base de données de PISA (ou de
182: n’importe quel autre évaluation internationale), particulièrement lorsqu’il s’agit des pays en voie de
183: développement ayant des systèmes d’éducation en expansion.

253: Of the OECD countries that participate in the Programme for International Student As sessment
254: (PISA), Turkey has one of the lowest levels of performance and the highest rates of improvement in PISA
255: scores (2003 -2012) . Between 2003 and 2012 Turkey managed to improve learning outcomes by almost a
256: full year of learning in Mathematics (25 poin ts) while also decreasing the achievement gap between rich
257: and poor (equity). While this is an impressive achievement in and of itself, it does not highlight the drastic
258: expansions in access and attainment – and therefore PISA sample eligibility – that hav e taken place over
259: the same period. PISA is supposed to “provide an assessment of the cumulative yield of education and
260: learning at a point at which most young adults are still enrolled in initial education” (OECD and UNESCO -
261: UIS, 2003, p. 249) . While this enrolment criterion is true for most OECD countries, where at least 80% of
262: 15-16 year -olds1 are eligible for PISA, historically this has not been the case for Turkey or a number of
263: other middle income countries such as Mexico, Brazil, Indonesia or Vietnam.
264: For example in PISA 2003 less than half (36 -45%2) of Turkish 15 -16 year -olds were actually eligible3
265: for the PISA sampling frame. To put this another way, PISA in Turkey in 2003 was representative of less
266: than half of the population of 15 -16 year-olds in the country. This is because PISA does not include out -of-
267: school 15 -16-year-olds (those who never enrol, have dropped out or left school with a primary school
268: diploma ) or those 15 -16-year-olds that are severely delayed ( those who are still enro lled in Grades 1 -6)
269: (OECD, 2005, p. 46) . Given that PISA -eligibility is not a random process – with under -performing and
270: poorer studen ts being more likely to be ineligible (Hanushek and Woessmann, 2011) – the research
271: question emerges: How does Turkey’s education system fare over the 2003 -2012 period if one takes into
272: account the changing proportions of PISA -eligible 15 -16-year-olds?
273: In this paper we attempt to answer this question by incorporating information on the level and
274: distribution of PISA -ineligible students from household -survey data at three points in time. Specifically we
275: use the 2003, 2008 and 2013 rounds of t he Turkish Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) data for
276: information on the out -of-school and delayed 15 -16-year-old population, and the 2003, 2009 and 2012
277: waves of PISA for the PISA -eligible 15 -16-year-old population. Doing so allows us to validly compare the
278: total population of 15 -16 year -olds across these three time periods.
279: Traditional analyses of Turkey’s PISA results ignore these large changes in PISA eligibility and thus
280: provide inaccurate estimates of achievement, progress and inequality, as we wil l show. Figure 1 below
281: provides just one indication of how large these changes have been. It reports on DHS data and shows that
282: the proportion of 15 -16 year -olds that were actually eligible for the PISA sample in Turkey (and thus the
284: 1 The sampling frame for the PISA assessment covers only those students that are aged 15 years and 3
285: (completed) months to 16 years and 2 (completed) months who are currently enrolled in Grade 7 or higher
286: (OECD, 2005, p. 46) . As is discussed later in the paper, using both 15 and 16 year -olds increases the
287: sample size (and therefore decreases the standard errors) when using the household su rvey data.
288: 2 The lower estimate reflects the OECD’s own “Coverage Index 3” for Turkey 2003 (OECD, 2004, p. 321 )
289: while the upper estimate is taken from the Turkish Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) data for 2003.
290: Both data sources are discussed further in the paper.
291: 3 For ease of reading, throughout this paper I use ‘sample eligibility’ and ‘sample coverage’ interchangeably.
292: In the technical terms used by the OECD these are marginally different because sample coverage is sample
293: eligibility minus legitimate exclusions. These exclusions might be school -level exclusions (for inaccessibly
294: remote schools), or for students that are intellectually disabled, for example. These exclusions are almost
295: always less than 5% in developing countries (OECD, 2012b: 149). Thus wherever the paper refers to
296: cove rage it should technically be read as “coverage after exclusions among those eligible.”
298: EDU/WKP(2017)3
299: 8 percentage of the popu lation that PISA is representative of) nearly doubled from 45% in 2003, to 80% in
300: 2013, with large declines in the proportion of out-of-school 15 -16 year -olds and those who had severely
301: delayed grade progression.

306: Structure of the paper
307: The analysis begins by presenting a short review of some of the existing Turkish PISA literature,
308: drawing specific attention to shortcomings around inter -temporal comparisons in Turkey. Thereafter the
309: method used in the paper to combine DHS and PISA data – first employed by Spaull and Taylor (2015) - is
310: discussed, and the two sources of data are reviewed. The main results are presented in Figures 2 -7 and
311: Tables 2 -9, each providing a breakdown in access, attainment and quality for 2003, 2009 and 2012 for the
312: country as a whole, as well as for three demographic sub -groups defined by: gender, socio -economic status
313: and a gender -socio -economic -status interaction. By assuming that PISA -ineligible 15 -16 year -olds would
314: not have reached PISA proficiency Level 2 in either Reading or Mathematics – a relatively conservative
315: assumption - it becomes possible to create what we ca ll an “access -to-literacy” and “access -to-numeracy”
316: rate. This is the proportion of the total population of 15 -16 year -olds that acquire PISA Level 2 in Reading
317: (functional literacy) and Mathematics (functional numeracy). After a discussion of the main fin dings we
318: ask whether or not the above assumption is valid and present some empirical evidence and sensitivity
319: analysis in support of it, as well as a penultimate section on the limitations of the present study. The
320: concluding section highlights the contrib ution that this analysis makes both to our understanding of the
323: EDU/WKP(2017)3
324: 9 Turkish education system between 2003 and 2012, and also to the need for a more circumspect approach
325: when interpreting PISA results in countries with large and changing percentages of ineligib le 15 -16 year -
326: olds.
327: LITERATURE
328: There is a large body of uncontested research showing that Turkey has made significant progress in
329: terms of learning outcomes, access and attainment. In a recent study, Köseleci (2015) provides a succinct
330: overview of the Tu rkish education literature over the last 15 years, and shows that while there has been
332: gender gap (Tanse l, 2002) , these reforms have been less successful in the eastern and south eastern regions
333: of Turkey (Kırdar, 2009; O’Dwyer, Aksit, and Sands, 2010) .
334: In almost all cases, this rapid expansion was a direct response to deliberate policy initiatives such as
335: ‘Rapid Coverage for Compulsory Education Program of 1997’ where over 1 million students enrolled in
336: Grades 1 -8 in the six year period 1997 -2002 (Dülger, 2004) , as well as progress in equity in response to the
337: Ninth Development Plan (covering 2007 -2013) which “prioritizes girls and students in rural areas and
343: Erbaş, 2010; Kırdar, 2009; Köseleci, 2015; Rankin and Aytac, 2006) .
344: PISA and sample eligibility
345: Since its inception in 2000, PISA has acquired global significance with approximately 70
346: countries/economies participating in the latest round (2015). To that end, some have argued that PISA “has
347: become the world’s premier yardstick for evaluating the qual ity, equity and efficiency of school systems”
348: (OECD, 2012b, p. 3) . While this may be true for most OECD countries, where PISA is representative of
349: more than 80% of 15 -16 year -olds, PISA may be a less accurate yardstick in Turkey where, historically, a
350: substantially lower proportion of 15 -16 year -olds were actually eligibl e for PISA. The same can be said for
351: other middle -income countries such as Mexico, Brazil, Indonesia and Vietnam.
352: Discussions around sample coverage in international assessments have been around for over two
353: decades. For example, Rotberg (1995, p. 1446) argues that:
354: “The basic problem is student selectivity: The fewer the students who take the test, the higher the
358: More recently, Ferreira and Gignoux explain that:
359: “Selection would not be a problem if one were interested exclusively in the performance of 15
360: year-olds that are in school, and within a reasonable range of their expected grade of attendanc e.
366: Ludger Woessman (2011 : 79), explain why this is the case: “Non -random differences in patterns of school
367: enrolment, sample exclusions, and non -response are clearly able to influence rankings of countries on
368: international league tables of average student achievement.” Following their analysis of TIMSS 1995,
369: 1999, and 2003, as well as PISA 2000 and 2003, they conclude as follows:
370: “We find that countries having more schools and students excluded from the targeted sample,
371: having schools and students who are less likely to participate in the test, and having higher
372: overall school enrolment at the relevant age level tend to perform better on the international
373: tests” (Hanushek and Woessmann, 2011 :79).
374: While Hanushek and Woessman do flag the issue of sample c overage, they are also at pains to explain
375: that accounting for these differences does not affect the conclusion that academic achievement is an
376: important determinant in economic growth regressions. However, this is not the case in the education
377: literature, at least not for Turkey. The findings presented in this paper show that the conclusions on both
378: educational inequality and progress change substantially when accounting for PISA -ineligible students in
379: Turkey.
380: The OECD is not unaware of the issues relatin g to sample representivity. In fact, in all PISA reports
381: and technical reports the OECD includes a variety of coverage indices, including ‘Coverage Index 3’
382: which is “the proportion of the national population of 15 year -olds covered by the non -excluded por tion of
383: the student sample” (OECD, 2014b, p. 177) . Or phrased differently, the proportion of the national 15 year
384: old populatio n (in and out of school) that is represented in the PISA sample. Although this statistic is
385: readily available for all countries and all rounds of PISA, very few researchers have analysed it or
386: discussed the implications for high, low or changing levels of this index. The only substantive treatment of
387: this issue (prior to the PISA 2015 report which will be discussed later) can be found in a single paragraph
388: found in all PISA reports from 2003 onwards, and in Volume II of the 2012 PISA Report in a section tit led
389: “Providing Access to Schooling to All 15 -year-olds” (OECD, 201 2b, p. 53) . The paragraph in question is
390: included verbatim below:
391: “This high level of coverage contributes to the comparability of the assessment results. For
392: example, even assuming that the excluded students would have systematically scored worse than
393: those who participated, and that this relationship is moderately strong, an exclusion rate in the
394: order of 5 per cent would likely lead to an overestimation of national mean scores of less than 5
395: score points … This assessment is based on the following cal culations: If the correlation between
396: the propensity of exclusions and student performance in 0.3, resulting mean scores would likely
397: to overestimated by 1 score point if the exclusion rate is 1 per cent, by 3 score points if the
398: exclusion rate is 5 per ce nt, and by 6 score points if the exclusion rate is 10 per cent. If the
399: correlation between the propensity of exclusions and student performance i s 0.5, resulting mean
400: scores would be overestimated by 1 score point if the exclusion rate is 1 per cent, by 5 score
401: points if the exclusion rate is 5 per cent, and by 10 score points if the exclusion rate is 10 per
402: cent. For this calculation, a model was employed that assumes a bivariate normal distribution for
403: the propensity to participate and performance” (OECD, 2004, p. 325)
404: Although this is taken from the PISA 2003 report, it can also be found in the PISA 2006, 2009 and
405: 2012 ( Vol. II) reports (on pages 24, 176 and 151 respectively). What this simulation seems to imply is that
406: correcting for those omitted from the PISA sample would not make much of a difference for most
407: countries. Indeed, this is explicitly stated in t he PISA 2012 (Volume II) report: “Most countries would
409: EDU/WKP(2017)3
410: 11 show no or very small changes in average performance after taking into account the performance of those
411: outside the school system” (OECD, 2012b, p. 53) . It goes on to mention five countries/economies where
412: changes in performance are largest: Hong Kong (China ), Mac ao(China ), Mexico, Shanghai -China, Turkey
413: and Viet Nam, but stresses that in all cases except Viet Nam and Macao (China ) this correction would not
414: change the ranking by much. This conclusion is drawn from Table II.2.12 in the annex of the PISA 2012
415: (Volume II) report which compares coverage rates between 2003 and 2012 and simulates how the scores
416: would change if those who were out of school achieved at Level 1 (OECD, 2012b, p. 210) .
417: Unfortunately the Coverage Index 3 (CI -3) values for PISA 2003 that were used in the PISA 2012
418: calculations described above are the i ncorrect figures. There is an error in the calculation of the CI -3
419: figures presented in the PISA 2003 report (OECD, 2004, p. 322) , and these differ from the (correct) figures
420: presented in the PISA 2003 Technical Report (OECD, 2005, p. 166)4. To provide an indication of how
421: large the differences are between the (incorrect) CI -3 figures of the PISA 2003 Report and the (correct) CI -
422: 3 figures of the PISA 2003 Technical Report, one can look at four countries. The correct figures are as
423: follows ( with incorrect figures in brackets): Indonesia 46% (73%), Brazil 54% (65%), Mexico 49% (58%),
424: and Turkey 36% (54%). Clearly the incorrect figures are much higher and therefore all analyses based on
425: these figures (like that in the PISA 2012 Report) will und erestimate how much of a difference including
426: the excluded students will make.
427: Using the OECD’s assumption of a 0.5 correlation between the propensity of exclusions and student
428: performance, Turkey’s 2003 PISA scores would drop by 64 points, or the equival ent of approximately two
429: years of schooling , if excluded 15 -16 year -olds were accounted for.
430: To provide some examples of just how low the coverage rates are in some countries, and how quickly
431: they have changed, Table 1 below provides the correct CI -3 rates for a sample of selected PISA countries
432: and years with very low levels of sample coverage (Germany and Canada are included as reference
433: countries). Note that Turkey has the lowest CI -3 rate of 36% in PISA 2003 an d that this nearly doubled to
434: 70% by P ISA 2015 . While a number of other countries have very low levels of sample coverage, the aim
435: here is to focus on Turkey to show how incorporating data on PISA -eligibility and coverage can shift
436: conclusions on education quite substantially. To calculate the se percentages the OECD divides the
437: weighted estimate of PISA -eligible students by the national population of 15 -year-olds, the latter being
438: provided by the National Project Manager in each country (OECD, 2014b, p. 177) . This figure is
439: calculated from the most recent census data, and may be somewhat dated depending on the year of the
440: census (OECD, 2012b, p. 211) .
441: Table 1. The percentage of the total 15 year old population covered by the PISA sampling frame (Coverage
442: Index 3) in selected countries

477: While the CI -3 statistic is useful, and provides a broad indication of how representative the PISA
478: sampling frame is, it tells us nothing about those youth that are omitted from the PISA sample. Looking at
479: CI-3 and PISA data alone cannot tell us how those who make it into the PISA sampling frame differ from
480: those that do not, or by how much. For example, they cannot tell us if girls or poorer students are more or
481: less likely to be included in the PISA sampling frame or how this has changed over time.
482: Although traditionally the OECD has under -emphasised the importance of sample coverage when
483: discussing trends and making policy recommendations, there are two notable exception s:
484: 1. The new OECD initiative aimed at developing countries : PISA for Development (PISA -D).
485: The aim of the PISA -D programme is to make PISA more relevant and accessible to a wider
486: range of countries. It is also “pioneering new methods and approaches to include out -of-school
487: youth in the assessment – a crucial first step towards addressing social exclusion” (OECD,
488: 2016b, p. 2) . This will take the form of a household -survey component which, together with the
489: school -based survey, c an provide a holistic picture of the achievement of 15 year -olds in the
490: PISA -D countries. There are no immediate plans to include this component for ‘traditional’ PISA
491: countries.
492: 2. The PISA 2015 Report . The PISA 2015 Report (OECD, 2016a) , launched in December 2016,
493: spends a considerable amount of time reporting on issues relating to sample eligibility,
494: representivity and coverage, which marks a break with all previous PISA reports. A simple
495: discourse analysis of the last five PISA reports makes this point abundantly clear. Analysing the
496: PISA 2003, 2006, 2009 and 2012 reports, one can find the word ‘coverage’ on 2 - 4% of the total
497: pages5. This is in stark contrast to the PISA 2015 Report where the word appears on 32% of the
498: report’s pages and has a number of dedicated sections to coverage and related topics of equity.
499: The report goes further to explain how this has the potential to impact representivity: “ Overall,
500: there are 20 countries in PISA 2015 where less than 80% of 15 -year-olds are enrolled in school
501: and eligible to participate in PISA. This implies that PISA results for these countries are not fully
502: representative of their populatio ns of 15 -year-olds. It also signals that these school systems face
503: serious challenges in becoming more inclusive and equitable ” (OECD, 2016a, p. 210) . While
504: resident at the OECD headquarters in Paris in 2016 I presented this research twice to the OECD
506: 5 The exact number of pages that the word ‘coverage’ can be found relative to the total number of pages in
507: the report for each year are as follows: 2003 (8/471), 2006 (8/390), 2009 (10/276), 2012 (11/564), and
508: 2015 (160/494).
510: EDU/WKP(2017)3
511: 13 staff members tasked with writing the PISA 2015 Report, and argued for its inclusion in the
512: (then) upcoming PISA report. It would seem that the present research, together with other reports
513: (Bloem, 2015; Lockheed, Prokic -Breuer, and Shadrova, 2015) have shifted the conversation
514: somewhat. While this is a welcome and important addition to the PISA reports, and one for
515: which the OECD should be congratulated, it will take some time before there is widespread
516: recognition that comparisons between countries with very different coverage rates are
517: problematic. In a recent World Bank working paper titled “Unravelling a Secret: Vietnam’s
518: Outstanding Performance on the PISA Test”, Parandekar and Sedmik (2016) put forward an
519: impressive list of possible explanations for Viet Nam’s high scores in PISA without once
520: mentioning that more 15 year -olds had been excluded from the PISA sampling frame in Viet
521: Nam than in any other country. A quite serious omission given that about half of all 15 year -olds
522: are not represented by the PISA sample in the country. For the remainder of this paper we will
523: focus on Turkey exclusively.
524: It is not only the OECD that has , up until very recent ly, given insufficient attention to the issues of
525: sample eligibility in Turkey (or other countries). For example, the World Bank (2005) in their ‘Education
526: Sector Study’ of Turkey ma de little mention of the PISA 2003 Turkey sampling issue. For example
527: throughout the report the PISA results are generalised to all Turkish 15 year -olds when in fact only 36% of
528: Turkish 15 year -olds were not covered in the PISA sample, according to the OECD. One excerpt from the
529: World Bank’s study: “Over half (55%) of Turkey’s 15 -year-old students were unable to perform above
530: level 1” (2005, p. 17). This does not take into account that nearly one in five 15 -16 year old students in
531: Turkey were still in school but were ineligible for PISA in 2003 because they were severely delayed, as
532: will be shown later in this paper using household survey data .
533: In the peer -reviewed academic literature there is very little discussion of the impact of sample
534: eligibility on PISA results. One exception is a recent short article titled “PISA for low - and middle -income
535: countries” where Bloem (2015, p. 485) explains that “High levels of out -of-school children mean PISA
536: cannot capture a representative sample of the 15 -year-old population. Measures, most importantly equity
537: measures, would be less meaningful if most disadvantaged students were no longer in school at this age,
538: and the PISA results would refer to an already relatively privileged student population” (see Bloem, 2013
539: for a more detailed discussion) .
540: Looking at Turkey specifically, in a recent paper titled “A critique of the interpretation and utili sation
541: of PISA results in Turkey” Gür, Çelik and Özoglu (2012) do not mention that less than half of Turki sh 15
542: year-olds were actually eligible for PISA 2003 in the first place, which is perhaps the most self -evident
543: critique. Similarly Alacaci and Erbas (2010) analyse PISA 2006 data and focus on inequality but do not
544: mention that less than half of 15 year -olds (CI3=47%) were eligible for the PISA sample in Turkey in
545: 2006. In fact they state that “ the Turkish sample is representative of the 15 -year-old students as the index
546: of coverage of the national enrolled -in-schools population was .98 (out of 1)” (Alacacı and Erbaş, 2010, p.
547: 184), citing Coverage Index 2. However Coverage Index 2 only recor ds eligible students and thus excludes
548: 15-16 year old students that are still enrolled but are in Grade 1 -6 (OEC D, 2004, p. 323) . In 2008 about
549: 18% of Turkish 15 -16 year -olds were still in school but were ineligible for PISA due to delayed
550: progression (according to the Turkish DHS 2008). The same can be said for a more recent analysis by
551: Özdemir (2016) on “Equity in the Turkish Education System” which analyses PISA 2012 data, which
552: again, is only representative of two of every three (CI3=68%) 15 -16 year -olds, according to the OECD.
553: The only Turkish study that we are aware o f that specifically mentions the lack of PISA coverage as a
554: point of concern is that of Köseleci and Şaşmaz (2011, p. 128) who caution that “it should be kept in mind
555: that PISA does not cover all 15 -year-old children but students of this age.” They use the 2009 Household
556: Labor Survey and conclude that “only 55 -60 percent of all 15 -year-olds in Turkey attend school regularly.”
557: This figure is relatively close to the 57% Coverage Index 3 estimated by the OECD (2012a, p. 121) and the
559: EDU/WKP(2017)3
561: using the 2008 DHS. These two authors argue that “When children who are not enrolled in schools, who
562: are most likely at a lower knowledge and skill level are also a ccounted for, Turkey’s average performance
563: and level distribution will be worse than what is reflected by PISA scores” (Köseleci and Sasmaz, 2011, p.
564: 128). Unfortunately the authors do not atte mpt to estimate how much lower Turkey’s performance would
565: be if these out of school youth were accounted for. The present analysis goes some way to answering this
566: question by combining both household survey and PISA data. The method for doing so is describ ed in the
567: next section.
568: METHOD
569: In PISA participating countries with low coverage rates, i n order to gain an accurate picture of school
570: system success and how this has changed over time , it becomes necessary to utili se (1) househo ld survey
571: data on enrolment and progression in conjunction with (2) PISA data on achievement for those in the
572: sampling frame. Doing so allows one to disaggregate the total number of 15 -16 year -old youth into four
573: mutually -exclusive categories:
579: 4. Those who e nrol remain in school and reach at least Grade 7 by the time they are 15 -16 years old
580: (i.e. those who make it into the PISA sampling frame).
581: In order to do this one needs two data -sources: (1) a measure of educational enrolment and attainment
582: by age, and (2) a measure of learning outcomes at a particular age (15 -16 years of age in the case of PISA).
583: The reason for this is that cross -national studies of educational achievement such as PISA typically use
584: schools as the sampling unit and thus cannot provide i nformation on the out -of-school population.
585: Information on topics such as non -enrolment, progression and dropout by gender, and particularly socio -
586: economic status, are also not usually available in government administrative data (see the online appendix
587: of Spaull and Taylor, 2015 for a full discussion) . In a similar vein, household surveys such as the DHS
588: have only rudiment ary assessments of learning outcomes, or none at all. Combining data from household
602: (variable names in brackets): age ( HV105 ), gender ( HV104 ), highest education level ( HV106 ), highest
603: grade in highest level ( HV107 ), current enrolment ( HV121 ). As well as the following weighting variables:
604: sample weight ( HV005 ), primary sampling unit ( HV021 ) and strata6 (HV023 ). All estimates were
605: calculated for the three sub -groups of gender (boys and girls), socio -econ omic status (Poorest 40%, Middle
606: 40%, and richest 20%), and a gender -socio -economic -status interaction (i.e. Poorest 40% who are also
608: Step two - Learning outcomes:
609: Calculate the p roportion of PISA students that attain some recognised benchmark in Reading and
610: Mathematics. For the present estimates of learning outcomes we use PISA proficiency Level 2 in Reading
611: and Mathematics. Level 2 is defined by the OECD as the baseline level of proficiency for all domains
612: (OECD, 2014a, p. 69) , what we refer to here as ‘functional literacy’ and functional numeracy’ respectively.
613: For example, in Reading the OECD (2010, p. 13) note that: “Level 2 can be considered a baseline level of
614: proficiency at which students begin to demonstrate the Reading competencies that will enable them to
615: participate effectively and productively in life. Students who do not reach Level 2 have difficu lties locating
619: We calculate the proportion of students reaching Level 2 for the three sub -groups identified above. To do
620: so we use the STATA program me repest (Avvisati and Keslair, 2014) with five plausible values for each
621: year and each domain. The program me was designed specifically for use with OECD data, including PISA,
622: and produces estimates that account for the complex survey design of PISA by using replicate weights.
623: Step three - Combining access and quality:
624: Once there are estimates of both learning outcomes for 15 -16 year -olds (PISA) and an indicator of the
625: proportion of students that were eligible for the PISA sampling frame (u sing DHS), one can calculate
626: ‘access -to-literacy’ and ‘access -to-numeracy’ rates. These are the proportions of a cohort of all youth aged
627: 15-16 years that achieve Level 2 in PISA Reading and Mathematics respectively. If one assumes that
628: students that are i neligible for PISA (either due to non -enrolment, dropout, graduating with a primary
629: qualification, or extremely delayed progression) and would not have achieved Level 2 in either Reading or
630: Mathematics, then one can simply multiply the PISA Literacy Rate ( Level 2 or above ) with the proportion
631: of 15 -16 year -olds that were eligible for the PISA sampling frame (taken from DHS). For example in 2003,
632: of the students that took the PISA Reading test, 63% achieved Level 2 or above. Yet according to the
633: Turkish DHS 2003, only 45% of 15 -16 year -olds were actually eligible for PISA in the first place. Thus the
634: access -to-literacy rate would be 28% (63% x 45%). That is to say that when looking at the population of
635: 15-16 year -olds as a whole (both in and out of school) ; only 28% would have reached PISA Level 2 or
636: higher in 2003. In all cases standard errors are reported for both PISA estimates and DHS estimates
637: (Tables 2 -9). For the combined statistic of access -to-literacy and access -to-numeracy the two standard
638: errors nee d to be combined in some way. Given that PISA and DHS use different samples in their surveys ,
639: the samples are independent and thus the composite standard errors are simply the square root of the sum
641: 6 For DHS 2008 the ‘HV023’ variable is missing and the strata must be created using unique values of
645: EDU/WKP(2017)3
646: 16 of the squared standard errors of each sample, i.e. (𝑆𝐸1𝐷𝐻𝑆2+ 𝑆𝐸2𝑃𝐼𝑆𝐴2)1/2 where the first element is the
647: standard error from DHS and the second element the standard error from PISA.
648: Step four – Correcting for differential access by socio -economic status:
649: Step three above illustrates how to calculat e access -to-literacy at the country level, and can also be
650: applied to males and females separately. However, existing literature has shown that there are large wealth -
651: based differentials in both learning outcomes (OECD, 2012b) and attainment (Filmer and Pritchett, 1999) .
652: Therefore one cannot simply multiply the literacy rat e for the poorest 40% of students in PISA with the
653: access rate of the poorest 40% of 15 -16 year -olds in the DHS because they do not represent the same
654: underlying population. The poorest 40% of 15 -16 year -olds in the DHS represent the poorest 40% of 15 -16
655: year-olds in the country . Whereas the poorest 40% of the PISA sample represent the poorest 40% of 15 -16
656: year-olds that were still in school and in Grade 7 or higher (i.e. eligible for PISA). These are two,
657: sometimes very different, groups.
658: This is made cle arer using a hypothetical example (see Spaull and Taylor, 2015 for a similar
659: example) . Assume that there are 1 ,000 15 -16 year -olds and that the national percentage that is eligible for
660: PISA is 85%, and that this proportion differs by socio -economic status. Among the poorest 400 15 -16
661: year-olds only 300 ar e eligible for PISA (75% eligibility rate), among the middle 400 15 -16 year -olds only
662: 350 are eligible for PISA (87.5% eligibility rate), and among the wealthiest 200 15 -16 year -olds all are
663: eligible for PISA (100% eligibility rate). Under these conditions , the national percentage of 15 -16 year -
664: olds that are eligible for the PISA sampling frame would be 85% ([300 + 350 + 200]/1,000). If one were to
665: ignore the difference in PISA eligibility and simply calculate the poorest 40%, middle 40% and richest
666: 20% of 15-16 year -olds using the 850 students that actually make it into the PISA sampling frame, one
667: would get categories of 340 students in the poorest 40%, 340 students in the middle 40%, and 170 students
668: in the richest 20% category. However, only 300 of the 8 50 students are actually from the poorest 40% of
669: households, not 340, and 200 students come from the richest 20% of households, not 170. Thus if one did
670: not apply a correction to account for the difference in PISA eligibility by wealth the result would
671: overestimate the access of the poorest 40% of 15 -16 year -olds.
672: To overcome the above issue we apply a correction that amounts to a reweighting of the PISA sample
673: based on the PISA -eligibility of each group found in DHS. This creates comparable groups in DHS and
674: PISA. Analogously, this can be thought of as calculating new socio -economic -status -grouping cut points in
675: the PISA sample to match those in the DHS sample . We first order the distribution of students in PISA by
676: the ‘family wealth’ variable created by t he OECD (2014b , p. 316) and then split this distribution according
677: to the PISA -eligibility rates of each wealth group found in the DHS7. This can be shown mathematically by
678: the following formula :
680: 7 For Turkey DHS 2003 and 2013 we use the wealth quintiles created by DHS (HV270). For DHS 2008 the
681: variable for wealth quintiles (HV270) significantl y underperforms a standard asset index created using
682: Multiple Correspondence Analysis (MCA) on 22 possession variables SH144B -SH144Y which includes a
686: 2008. We believe it is preferable to use the DHS’ own wealth quintiles where possible which is why we
687: used these in 2003 and 2013 but this is inadvisable for 2008. As a se nsitivity check we also created the
688: MCA asset index for 2003 and 2013 to see whether 15 -16 year olds were classified differently in each
689: wealth group (40:40:20) whether using the DHS’ wealth index or the MCA index. In 2003 90% of students
690: were classified i n the same category irrespective of wealth -quintile method. In 2013 the figure is 92% of
691: students.
702: 0.4∗𝑃𝐸𝑅 𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙)
703: Where PERpoor40 is the PISA Eligibility Rate for group 1 (poorest 40%), PERmid40 is the PISA
704: Eligibility Rate for group 2 (middle 40%), PERrich20 is the PISA Eligibility Rate for group 3 (richest
705: 20%), PERtotal is the national PISA Eligibility Rate. These eligibility rates are calcu lated from the DHS
706: sample as the percentage of 15 -16 year -olds that are still enrolled in Grade 7 or higher. The variable CNses
707: is the cumulative distribution of weighted 15 -16 year -olds in PISA sorted from poorest to wealthiest, using
708: the PISA Student Pop ulation Weight (W_FSTUWT). The first integral represents the PISA students that
709: correspond to the poorest 40% of students from DHS, the second integral represents the PISA students that
710: correspond to the middle 40% of the DHS population and the last integr al represents the PISA students that
711: correspond to the richest 20% of the DHS population.
712: Assumptions about the performance of PISA -ineligible students
713: The above relies on a key assumption that PISA -ineligible students would not have achieved Level 2
714: or higher if they were actually assessed. We believe that both the quantitative and qualitative literature
715: suggests that this is a reasonable assumption. Many students who dropout do so because they have failed
718: distribution before dropout (Lewin, 2007) . Finally, given t hat many of the students who are eligible for
719: PISA do not reach Level 2, it is unlikely that those who have dropped out would be a better performing
720: group than their peers who remain in school. An important fact about Turkey over the 2003 -2012 period is
721: that there was only 8 years of compulsory schooling and that those who completed the 8 years were
722: granted primary school degrees. In Turkey a number of youth leave school after receiving their primary
723: school qualification, either to start work or, frequently in the case of girls, to get married (Celik and
724: Lüküslü, 2010). Even in this situation there are selection effects in terms of who stays in schooling, who
725: leaves to work, and who leave to get married.
726: The existing academic literature supports these argu ments and shows that there are strong selection
727: effects involved in determining who goes to school and who stays in school, with the ‘strongest’ (i.e. the
728: wealthiest, most advantaged, and most able) remaining in the schooling system and progressing on tim e
729: (Lambin, 1995; Lewin and Little, 2011; Lewin, 2007) . The DHS data also confirm this. Among the richest
730: 20% of 15 -16 year -olds in Turkey 2003, only 8% had dropped out of scho ol compared to 49% among the
731: poorest 40% of 15 -16 year -olds (see Table 4). We believe it is reasonable to assume that there is a
732: monotonic relationship between performance and an ordinal measure of educational status. That is to say
733: that, on average and in the large majority of cases, those who are delayed would perform worse on PISA (if
734: they were assessed) than those who are not delayed; those who dropout would perform worse than those
735: who are merely delayed; and those who never enrol in school are likely to perform worst of all.
736: Given that PISA uses age -based sampling criteria rather than grade -based criteria, it becomes possible
737: to test this assumption empirically. Do students that are 1, 2 or 3 grades delayed perform worse on PISA
738: than those that do no t? Looking specifically at Turkey we find that delayed students that are in PISA
739: perform significantly worse than those that are not delayed. In the PISA 2003 Reading assessment, 63% of
740: non-delayed students (i.e. those with the modal grade) achieved Level 2 or higher compared to 13% of
741: those who were delayed by two or three years relative to the modal grade. We return to this issue later in
742: the paper when conducting robustness checks.
744: EDU/WKP(2017)3
747: For the estimates on access, attainment and progression we use the Turkish Demographic and Health
748: Survey (DHS) data from 2003, 2008 and 2013. The 2003 data was available from the DHS Program
749: website8. The 2008 and 20 13 DHS data are only available on the Hacettepe University Institute of
750: Population Studies website9. The DHS are an important source of data for public health and social science
751: research and are widely used in both fields, including in education. In our pr evious work (Spaull and
752: Taylor, 2015, p. 140) , we have discussed some of the benefits and limitations of using the DHS data over
753: other sour ces. Some of these benefits include:
754: 1. Self-reported enrolment and grade completion rates are often more accurate than administrative
755: records, the quality of which varies widely between countries (UNESCO Institute for Statistics,
756: 2010) . Unlike country -specific administrative data, the uniformity of the surveys means that DHS
760: data.
761: 3. Grade completion rates by age, which is one of the PISA sampling eligibility criteria, cannot be
762: calculated reliably using administrative education data.
763: Some of the limitations include the introduction of sampling errors, hous ehold non -response, and
764: exclusion of homeless children from the sampling frame, measurement error and problems with capturing
765: school attendance. However, given that DHS data have been used in hundreds of peer -reviewed academic
766: publications for a variety of purposes, including educational attainment (Filmer and Pritchett, 1999) and
767: enrolment (Hanushek and Woessmann, 2008) , we do not believe that any of these problems outweigh the
768: serious limitations of the alternatives.
769: The DHS is stratified using a combination of first-level NUTS10 regions, urban and rural variables, a
770: sub-divided Istanbul (slum and non -slum), and separ ate strata for metropolitan cities. This creates 40 strata
771: in DHS 2003 (Hacettepe -UIPS, 2003, p. 169) , and 39 strata in both DHS 2008 (Hacet tepe-UIPS, 2009, p.
772: 209), and DHS 2013 (Hacettepe -UIPS, 2014, p. 197) . All estimates presented in this paper have been
773: weighted and stratified in accordance with the various DHS reports11 using STATA’s svy command for
774: complex two -stage sampling.
775: The main aim of using a household survey data for the present analysis is to gain information on 15
776: year-olds that are ineligible for PISA, either due to delayed grade progression or because they are not
777: currently enrolled in school. One problem with using DHS data for this purpose is the number of 15 year -
778: olds included in the sur vey. The smaller the number , the less representative the data is and the larger the
779: standard errors associated with each estimate. To overcome this problem we have included both 15 year -
780: olds and 16 year -olds in the analysis. We do not believe that this is especially problematic given that PISA
782: 8 Access was formally requested and granted under Project Number: 906922.
784: 10 The ‘No menclature of Territorial Units for Statistics’ (NUTS) is a statistical classification of regions that is
785: used by member countries of the European Union and was introduced in Turkey in 2002.
786: 11 In DHS 2003 and 2013, strata are identified in variable ‘HV02 3’. For DHS 2008 the ‘HV023’ variable in
787: missing and the strata must be created using unique values of ‘HV005’.
789: EDU/WKP(2017)3
790: 19 does actually sample some 16 year -olds in addition to 15 year -olds12. In Turkey the number of 16 year -
791: olds in the PISA sample ranges from 27% (in 2009 and 2012) to 36% (in 2003). By including two age -
792: years one can bot h increase representivity and reduce the standard errors. Furthermore, the estimates from
793: a sensitivity analysis using only 15 year -olds does not differ substantially from those presented here.
794: National point estimates, as well as those for boys and girls, differ by less than three percentage points
795: when comparing 15 year -olds only and 15 and 16 year -olds together.
796: The total number of 15 -16 year -olds and the number of unique households from which they came
797: were as follows: In DHS 2003 there were 1,982 15-16 year -olds from 1 ,800 unique households. In DHS
798: 2008 there were 1,780 15-16 year -olds from 1 ,629 unique households. In DHS 2013 there were 1,646 15-
799: 16 year -olds from 1 ,535 households. Throughout this paper I use the de jure population of 15 -16 year -olds
800: found in the household survey, i.e. those who usually live in the selected household, rather than the de
801: facto population, i.e. those who spent the previous night in the household (Hacettepe -UIPS, 2003, p. 17) .
802: This is one way to further increase the sample size since the de jure population is slightly larger than the de
803: facto population.
804: It is worth highlighting one important feature of education statistics in Turkey13, and one that helps
805: explain the discrepancy between DHS estimates and OECD estimates of sample eligibility. Up until 2007
806: the Ministry of National Education (MoN E) used age -district level population projections based on Census
807: 2000. From 2007 the Ministry of Interior introduced the Address Based Population Registry System
808: (Turkish abbreviation: ADNKS). Once the MoNE started using ADNKS to compute enrolment rates i t
809: realised that they were drastically over -estimating the population of school -age children between 2000 and
810: 2006. Given that the OECD uses the population and enrolment estimates provided by country staff t his is
811: one of the explanations for the difference between the DHS estimates and the Coverage Index 3 estimates
812: in 2003. Consequently, the CI -3 statistics are not strictly comparable over time for Turkey, while the DHS
813: estimates should be comparable. A further reason why the OECD estimates might be lower t han the DHS
814: estimates is that the Coverage Index 3 statistic reports the rate of coverage after sample exclusions have
815: been taken into account. For example, if certain schools were ineligible for the sample because of conflict
816: or because they were too remo te, this would be reflected in the OECD estimates but not in the DHS
817: estimates.
820: the percentage of 15 -16 year -olds in each wave of the DHS are broken up into five mutually exclusive
821: categories: (1) those who never enrol and are therefore ineligible for the PISA sampling frame, (2) those
822: who have dropped out of school and are also ineligible, (3) those who are still in sch ool but are enrolled in
823: Grades 1 -6 and therefore ineligible for PISA, (4) those who are still enrolled in school and in Grade 7 or
825: 12 The sampling frame for the PISA assessment covers students that are aged 15 years and 3 (completed)
826: months to 16 years and 2 (completed) months who are currently enrolled in Grade 7 or higher (OECD,
827: 2005, p. 46) .

833: 20 higher (i.e. eligible for PISA) but who did not reach proficiency Level 2 (referred to as functionally
834: illiterate and functio nally innumerate for Reading and Mathematics), and (5) those who are eligible for
835: PISA and did reach Level 2 (referred to as functionally literate and functionally numerate). This last group
836: is said to have “access -to-literacy” or “access -to-numeracy” resp ectively. The tables provide the point
837: estimates and standard errors for the percentage of 15 -16 year -olds in DHS that were enrolled in Grade 7
838: or higher and were thus eligible for PISA (Table 2), those who never enrolled in school (Table 3), those
839: who left school (due to drop out or because they had acquired their primary school qualification) (Table 4),
840: and those who were still enrolled in Grades 1 -6 (Table 5). These last three groups are all ineligible for
841: PISA. Table 6 and Table 7 report the percentage of the PISA sample that acquired functional literacy
842: (Level 2 Reading) and functional numeracy (Level 2 Mathematics). These PISA estimates are the
843: ‘uncorrected’ estimates that match to those reported in the official OECD publications. Table 8 and Table 9

846: and also attained PISA proficiency Level 2 in Reading and Mathematics respectively. As has been
847: mentioned earlier, these estimates assume that those who are ineligible for PISA – because they are no
848: longer in school or are extreme ly delay ed – would not have reached Level 2 if they were assessed.
